Multivariate MM-estimators with auxiliary Scale for Linear Models with Structured Covariance Matrices
Hendrik Paul Lopuhaä

Abstract
We provide a unified approach to MM-estimation with auxiliary scale for balanced linear models with structured covariance matrices. This approach leads to estimators that are highly robust against outliers and highly efficient for normal data. These properties not only hold for estimators of the regression parameter, but also for estimators of scale invariant transformations of the variance parameters. Of main interest are MM-estimators for linear mixed effects models, but our approach also includes MM-estimators in several other standard multivariate models. We provide sufficient conditions for the existence of MM-functionals and MM-estimators, establish asymptotic properties such as consistency and asymptotic normality, and derive their robustness properties in terms of breakdown point and influence function. All the results are obtained for general identifiable covariance structures and are established under mild conditions on the distribution of the observations, which goes far beyond models with elliptically contoured densities.



1
Introduction

Linear models with a structured covariance are a generalization of traditional linear models where the residuals
are assumed to follow a specific covariance structure rather than being independent and identically distributed.
This approach is useful when the residuals are correlated or exhibit some form of structure that can’t be captured by simple uncorrelated noise.
These models are often used in cases like repeated measures, longitudinal data, and hierarchical structures,
where the observations within a group or over time might be more similar to each other than to observations from other groups or time points.
An example are linear mixed effects models, which explicitly account for both fixed effects (predictors whose effects are the same across all units) and random effects (predictors whose effects vary across groups or subjects).
In these models, the random effects together with the residuals yields a specific covariance structure depending
on a vector of unknown covariance parameters.

Maximum likelihood estimation has been studied by Hartley and Rao
[
12
]
,
Rao
[
28
]
, Laird and Ware
[
16
]
,
see also Fitzmaurice
et al

[
10
]
and Demidenko
[
8
]
.
To be resistant against outliers, robust methods have been investigated for linear mixed effects models
by Pinheiro
et al

[
26
]
,
Copt
[
5
]
,
Copt and Heritier
[
4
]
,
Heritier
et al

[
13
]
,
Agostinelli and Yohai
[
1
]
,
and Chervoneva and Vishnyakov
[
3
]
,
or for more general linear models with a structured covariance by Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
.
This often concerns S-estimators originally proposed by Rousseeuw and Yohai
[
29
]

for the multiple linear regression model.
These estimators have been extended to several multivariate statistical models
and can be viewed as smooth versions of the minimum volume ellipsoid estimator,
introduced by Rousseeuw
[
30
]
,
that are highly resistant against outliers.
However, one drawback of S-estimators is that they suffer from a low efficiency.

Some extensions have been proposed that inherit the robustness of the S-estimator,
but at the same time improve the efficiency.
Among them are the MM-estimators introduced by Yohai
[
36
]
for the multiple linear regression model.
The idea is to estimate the scale by means of a robust M-estimator, and then estimate
the regression parameter using a regression M-estimator with a different loss function
that yields better efficiency.
This idea has been extended in different ways to multivariate statistical models.
Lopuhaä
[
21
]
proposed a version for multivariate location,
Copt and Heritier
[
4
]
used the same approach to estimate the fixed effects
in a linear mixed effects model, and a similar method has been studied in Lopuhaä
[
18
]

for more general linear models with a structured covariance.
All these proposals use a robust estimator of the entire scatter matrix in the first step
and only allow efficiency improvement of the location or regression estimator.
Tatsuoka and Tyler
[
32
]
introduced a more extensive version of
multivariate MM-estimators for multivariate location and scatter,
being members of a broad class of multivariate M-estimators with auxiliary scale.
Their proposal only uses a robust M-estimator of the scale of the scatter matrix in the first step
and allows efficiency improvement of both the location estimator as well as the estimator
of the shape of the scatter matrix.
For this reason, this version of multivariate MM-estimators with auxiliary scale
is particularly useful for applications that require estimation of a covariance matrix.

The theory for these estimators is fairly limited.
Kudraszow and Maronna
[
15
]
study
MM-estimators with auxiliary scale for multivariate linear regression,
but no rigorous results are derived for the covariance MM-estimator.
Tatsuoka and Tyler
[
32
]
study existence of the corresponding MM-functionals,
but no attention is paid to the limiting behavior of the MM-estimators themselves.
As a basis for a robust PCA method, Salibián-Barrera
et al

[
31
]

use covariance MM-estimators and discusses their limiting behavior, but a rigorous derivation is missing.

In view of this, we provide a unified approach to MM-estimation with auxiliary scale in balanced linear models with structured covariance matrices.
The balanced setup is already quite flexible and includes several specific multivariate statistical models.
Of main interest are MM-estimators for linear mixed effects models, but our approach also includes
MM-estimators in several other standard multivariate models, such as
multivariate linear regression, and multivariate location and scatter.
We provide sufficient conditions for the existence of MM-functionals and MM-estimators,
establish their asymptotic properties, such as consistency and asymptotic normality,
and derive their robustness properties in terms of breakdown point and influence function.
All results are obtained for a large class of identifiable covariance structures, and are established under very mild conditions
on the distribution of the observations, which goes far beyond models with elliptically contoured densities.

The paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we explain the model in detail and
provide some examples of standard multivariate models that are included in our setup.
In Section
3
we define the MM-estimator and MM-functional
and in Section
4
we give conditions under which they exist.
In Section
5
we establish continuity of the MM-functional, which is then used to
obtain consistency of the MM-estimator.
Section
6
deals with the breakdown point.
Section
7
provides the preparation for Sections
8

and
9
in which we obtain the influence function and establish asymptotic normality.
Our results lead to single scalar indices for the asymptotic efficiency and the gross-error-sensitivity of
standardized components of the MM-estimators of the variance parameters.
In Section
10
we investigate the interplay between these two scalars at the multivariate
normal and Student distributions.
All proofs are available as supplemental material
[
19
]
.

2
Balanced linear models with structured covariances

We consider independent observations
(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{y}_{n},\mathbf{X}_{n})
,
for which we assume the following model

\mathbf{y}_{i}=\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta}+\mathbf{u}_{i},\quad i=1,\ldots,n,

(2.1)

where
\mathbf{y}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
contains repeated measurements for the
i
-th subject,

\bm{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
is an unknown parameter vector,

\mathbf{X}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times q}
is a known design matrix, and
the
\mathbf{u}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
are unobservable independent mean zero random vectors with
covariance matrix
\mathbf{V}\in\text{PDS}(k)
,
the class of positive definite symmetric
k\times k
matrices.
The model is balanced in the sense that all
\mathbf{y}_{i}
have the same dimension.
Furthermore, we consider a structured covariance matrix, that is,
the matrix
\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is a known function of unknown covariance parameters combined in a vector

\bm{\theta}\in\mathbf{\bm{\Theta}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{l}
.
We first discuss some examples that are covered by this setup
in the context of MM-estimators.

Example 1
.

An important case of interest is the (balanced) linear mixed effects model

\mathbf{y}_{i}=\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta}+\mathbf{Z}\bm{\gamma}_{i}+\bm{\epsilon}_{i}
, for

i=1,\ldots,n
.
This model arises from
\mathbf{u}_{i}=\mathbf{Z}\bm{\gamma}_{i}+\bm{\epsilon}_{i}
,
for
i=1,\ldots,n
,
where
\mathbf{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times g}
is known and
\bm{\gamma}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{g}
and
\bm{\epsilon}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
are independent mean zero random variables, with
unknown covariance matrices
\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{R}
, respectively.
In this case,
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{G}\mathbf{Z}^{T}+\mathbf{R}
and

\bm{\theta}=(\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{G})^{T},\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{R})^{T})^{T}
,
where

\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{A})=(a_{11},\ldots,a_{k1},a_{22},\ldots,a_{kk})

(2.2)

is the unique
k(k+1)/2
-vector that stacks the columns of the lower triangle elements of a symmetric matrix
\mathbf{A}
.
In full generality, the model is usually overparametrized and one may run into identifiability problems.
A more feasible example is obtained by taking
\mathbf{R}=\sigma_{0}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{k}
,

\mathbf{Z}=\left[\mathbf{Z}_{1}\,\cdots\,\mathbf{Z}_{r}\right]
and

\bm{\gamma}_{i}=(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}^{T},\ldots,\bm{\gamma}_{ir}^{T})^{T}
,
where the
\mathbf{Z}_{j}
’s are known
k\times g_{j}
design matrices and
the
\bm{\gamma}_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}^{g_{j}}
are independent mean zero random variables with covariance matrix
\sigma_{j}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{g_{j}}
,
for
j=1,\ldots,r
.
This leads to

\mathbf{y}_{i}=\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}\mathbf{Z}_{j}\bm{\gamma}_{ij}+\bm{\epsilon}_{i},\quad i=1,\ldots,n,

(2.3)

which was considered in Copt and Heritier
[
4
]
.
In this case,
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})=\sigma_{0}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}\sigma_{j}^{2}\mathbf{Z}_{j}\mathbf{Z}_{j}^{T}

and
\bm{\theta}=(\sigma_{0}^{2},\sigma_{1}^{2},\ldots,\sigma_{r}^{2})
.

Example 2
.

Another example of (
2.1
) is the multivariate linear regression model

\mathbf{y}_{i}=\mathbf{B}^{T}\mathbf{x}_{i}+\mathbf{u}_{i},\qquad i=1,\ldots,n,

(2.4)

considered in Kudraszow and Maronna
[
15
]
, where
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times k}
is a matrix of unknown parameters,

\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
is known,
and
\mathbf{u}_{i}
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
, are independent mean zero random variables with
covariance matrix
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbf{\Sigma}\in\text{PDS}(k)
.
In this case, the vector of unknown covariance parameters is given by

\bm{\theta}=\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
, where
\mathrm{vech}(\cdot)
is defined in (
2.2
).
The model can be obtained as a special case of (
2.1
), by taking

\mathbf{X}_{i}=\mathbf{x}_{i}^{T}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{k}
and
\bm{\beta}=\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{B}^{T})
, where
\mathrm{vec}(\cdot)
is the
k^{2}
-vector that stacks the columns of a matrix.
Clearly, the multiple linear regression model considered in Yohai
[
36
]
is a special case
of (
2.4
) with
k=1
.

Example 3
.

Also the multivariate location-scatter model, as considered in Lopuhaä
[
21
]
,
Tatsuoka and Tyler
[
32
]
, and
Salibián-Barrera et al
[
31
]
,
can be obtained as a special case of (
2.1
),
by taking
\mathbf{X}_{i}=\mathbf{I}_{k}
, the
k\times k
identity matrix.
In this case,
\bm{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
is the unknown location parameter and covariance matrix

\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbf{\Sigma}\in\text{PDS}(k)
, with
\bm{\theta}=\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
.
Note that this model can also be obtained as a special case of (
2.4
)
by taking
\mathbf{x}_{i}=1
and
\mathbf{B}^{T}=\bm{\beta}
.
This means that results in Kudraszow and Maronna
[
15
]
for model (
2.4
)
also apply to the multivariate location-scatter model.

Example 4
.

Model (
2.1
) also includes examples, for which
\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{n}
are generated by a time series.
An example is the case where
\mathbf{u}_{i}
has a covariance matrix with elements

v_{st}=\sigma^{2}\rho^{|s-t|}
,
for
s,t=1,\ldots,n
.
This arises when the
\mathbf{u}_{i}
’s are generated by an autoregressive process of order one.
The vector of unknown covariance parameters is
\bm{\theta}=(\sigma^{2},\rho)\in(0,\infty)\times(-1,1)
.
A general stationary process leads to

v_{st}=\theta_{|s-t|+1}
,
for
s,t=1,\ldots,n
,
in which case
\bm{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
where
\theta_{|s-t|+1}
represents the autocovariance over lag
|s-t|
.

Throughout the manuscript we will assume that the parameter
\bm{\theta}
is identifiable in the sense that,

\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1})=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{2})
implies
\bm{\theta}_{1}=\bm{\theta}_{2}
.
This is true for all models in
Examples
2
,

3
, and
4
.
This may not be true in general for the linear mixed effects model
in Example
1
with unknown
\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{G})
and
\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{R})
.
For linear mixed effects models
in (
2.3
),
identifiability of
\bm{\theta}=(\sigma_{0}^{2},\sigma_{1}^{2},\ldots,\sigma_{r}^{2})

holds for particular choices of the design matrices

\mathbf{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{Z}_{r}
.

3
Definitions

We start by representing our observations as points in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
in the following way.
For
r=1,\ldots,k
, let
\mathbf{x}_{r}^{T}
denote the
r
-th row of the
k\times q
matrix
\mathbf{X}
,
so that
\mathbf{x}_{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
.
We represent the pair
\mathbf{s}=(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})
as an element in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
defined
by
\mathbf{s}^{T}=(\mathbf{y}^{T},\mathbf{x}_{1}^{T},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{k}^{T})
.
In this way our observations can be represented as
\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{n}
, with
\mathbf{s}_{i}=(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
.

Similar to MM-estimators for multiple linear regression introduced by Yohai
[
36
]
,
MM-estimators for
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta})
are based on two loss functions.
We require the following conditions for a loss function
\rho
:

(R1)

\rho
is symmetric around zero with
\rho(0)=0
and
\rho
is continuous at zero;

(R2)

There exists a finite constant
c>0
, such that
\rho
is non-decreasing on
[0,c]
and constant on
[c,\infty)
;

(R3)

\rho
is continuous and strictly increasing on
[0,c]
.

In comparison with other proposals for MM-estimators,
conditions (R1)-(R3) imply condition (A1) in Yohai
[
36
]

and Definition 2 in Kudraszow and Maronna
[
15
]
.
The conditions are similar to the ones in Tatsuoka and Tyler
[
32
]

and the ones in Salibián-Barrera
et al

[
31
]
.

STAGE 1:

Let
\bm{\beta}_{0,n}
and
\bm{\theta}_{0,n}
be initial (high breakdown) estimators for
\bm{\beta}
and
\bm{\theta}
,
and consider the shape estimator
\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{0,n})
, where for
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}
,

\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta})=\frac{\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})}{|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})|^{1/k}},

(3.1)

where
|\mathbf{A}|
denotes the determinant of
\mathbf{A}
.

STAGE 2:

Let
\rho_{0}
satisfy (R1)-(R3) and
determine
\sigma_{n}
by solving
\sigma
from

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{0}\left(\frac{\displaystyle\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta}_{0,n})^{T}\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{0,n})^{-1}(\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta}_{0,n})}}{\sigma}\right)=b_{0},

(3.2)

where
0<b_{0}<\sup\rho_{0}
.

STAGE 3:

Let
\rho_{1}
satisfy (R1)-(R3) and is such that

\frac{\rho_{1}}{\sup\rho_{1}}\leq\frac{\rho_{0}}{\sup\rho_{0}}.

(3.3)

For
(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{C})\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\text{PDS}(k)
, define

R_{n}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{C})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{1}\left(\frac{\displaystyle\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{C}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta})}}{\sigma_{n}}\right),

(3.4)

and let

\mathfrak{D}=\left\{(\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma})\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\bm{\Theta}:\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})\in\text{PDS}(k)\text{ with }|\mathbf{V}(\gamma)|=1\right\}
.
Let
(\bm{\beta}_{1,n},\bm{\gamma}_{n})\in\mathfrak{D}
be any local minimum of

R_{n}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))

that satisfies

R_{n}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))\leq R_{n}(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{0,n})),

(3.5)

where
\bm{\Gamma}
is defined in (
3.1
).
Update the covariance estimator by
\mathbf{V}_{1,n}=\sigma_{n}^{2}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{n})

and update the estimator
\bm{\theta}_{1,n}
for the vector of covariance parameters as the solution of

\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})=\sigma_{n}^{2}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{n}).

(3.6)

The idea is to choose estimators
\bm{\beta}_{0,n}
and
\bm{\theta}_{0,n}
with high breakdown point
and to choose loss function
\rho_{0}
suitably, so that
\sigma_{n}
will also have high breakdown point.
Estimators
\bm{\beta}_{1,n}
and
\bm{\theta}_{1,n}
will be shown to inherit this high breakdown point,
but at the same time the regression estimator
\bm{\beta}_{1,n}
as well as the estimator of shape
\mathbf{V}_{1,n}/|\mathbf{V}_{1,n}|^{1/k}

and the estimator of direction
\bm{\theta}_{1,n}/\|\bm{\theta}_{1,n}\|
will also have high efficiency relative to the least squares estimators
by suitable choice of loss function
\rho_{1}
.
We will show that the absolute minimum of
R_{n}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
with
|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})|=1
exists.
Clearly, this absolute minimum satisfies (
3.5
).
However, any local minimum satisfying (
3.5
), will also be an MM-estimator
with high breakdown point and high efficiency.

Examples of loss functions satisfying (
3.3
) can be constructed from
Tukey’s bi-weight, defined as

\rho_{\mathrm{B}}(s;c)=\begin{cases}s^{2}/2-s^{4}/(2c^{2})+s^{6}/(6c^{4}),&|s|\leq c\\
c^{2}/6&|s|>c.\end{cases}

(3.7)

The functions
\rho_{0}(s)=\rho_{\mathrm{B}}(s;c_{0})
and
\rho_{1}(s)=\rho_{\mathrm{B}}(s;c_{1})
, for
0<c_{0}\leq c_{1}<\infty
,
satisfy (R1)-(R3) as well as (
3.3
).
Examples of
\bm{\beta}_{0,n}
and
\bm{\theta}_{0,n}
with high breakdown point are the S-estimators
discussed in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
defined with
\rho_{0}(s)=\rho_{\mathrm{B}}(s;c_{0})
.
Small values of the cut-off constant
c_{0}
will then correspond to a high breakdown point.

This definition of MM-estimators for the linear mixed effects model differs from the ones in Lopuhaä
[
18
]
and
Copt and Heritier
[
4
]
, where
the entire initial covariance matrix is used as initial estimator,
which then involves minimization of
R_{n}
over
\bm{\beta}
only.
The current definition only uses the univariate estimator
\sigma_{n}
for the scale parameter

|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})|^{1/(2k)}
as an auxiliary statistic.
The advantage is that this version of the MM-estimator allows for improvement of the efficiency of both the regression estimator
as well as the estimator of the shape component of
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
and the estimator of the direction component of
\bm{\theta}
.

The corresponding MM-functionals are defined similarly.

STAGE 1:

Let
\bm{\beta}_{0}(P)
and
\bm{\theta}_{0}(P)
be initial functionals and consider the shape functional
\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))
, where
\bm{\Gamma}
is defined in (
3.1
).

STAGE 2:

Let
\rho_{0}
satisfy (R1)-(R3) and
determine
\sigma(P)
by solving
\sigma
from

\int\rho_{0}\left(\frac{\displaystyle\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0}(P))^{T}\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0}(P))}}{\sigma}\right)\,\text{d}P(\mathbf{s})=b_{0},

(3.8)

where
0<b_{0}<\sup\rho_{0}
.

STAGE 3:

Let
\rho_{1}
satisfy (R1)-(R3) and is such that (
3.3
) holds.
For
(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{C})\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\text{PDS}(k)
, define

R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{C})=\int\rho_{1}\left(\frac{\displaystyle\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{C}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})}}{\sigma(P)}\right)\text{d}P(\mathbf{s}).

(3.9)

Let
(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}(P))\in\mathfrak{D}
be any local minimum of

R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))

that satisfies

R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))\leq R_{P}(\bm{\beta}_{0}(P),\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))),

(3.10)

where
\bm{\Gamma}
is defined in (
3.1
).
Update the covariance functional by

\mathbf{V}_{1}(P)=\sigma^{2}(P)\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P))

and update the functional
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)
for the vector of covariance parameters as the solution of

\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})=\sigma^{2}(P)\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P)).

(3.11)

Let
\mathbb{P}_{n}
be the empirical measure corresponding to observations
(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{y}_{n},\mathbf{X}_{n})
.
We assume that the initial functionals
\bm{\beta}_{0}(\cdot)
and
\bm{\theta}_{0}(\cdot)
are such that

(\bm{\beta}_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{n}),\bm{\theta}_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{n}))=(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\theta}_{0,n}),

(3.12)

where
(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\theta}_{0,n})
are the initial estimators for
\bm{\beta}
and
\bm{\theta}
.
Examples for which (
3.12
) holds, are the S-functionals discussed in
Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
defined with loss function
\rho_{0}
.
If (
3.12
) holds, then

\sigma_{n}=\sigma(\mathbb{P}_{n})
,
(\bm{\beta}_{1,n},\bm{\gamma}_{n})=(\bm{\beta}_{1}(\mathbb{P}_{n}),\bm{\gamma}(\mathbb{P}_{n}))
,
and
\bm{\theta}_{1,n}=\bm{\theta}_{1}(\mathbb{P}_{n})
.

The definition of MM-estimators and corresponding functionals in our current setup
includes several special cases that are already available in the literature.
For the multivariate location and scatter model of Example
3
,
our MM-functionals
\bm{\beta}_{1}(P)
and
\sigma^{2}(P)\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P))

coincide with the multivariate location and scatter M-functionals
with auxiliary scale
\sigma(P)
, as discussed in Tatsuoka and Tyler
[
32
]
.
When, in addition,
\bm{\beta}_{0,n}
and
\mathbf{C}_{0,n}=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{0,n})
are the S-estimators for location and scatter defined by means of
\rho_{0}
,
then our MM-estimators
\bm{\beta}_{1,n}
and
\sigma_{n}^{2}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{n})
coincide with the MM-estimators for location and scatter considered in Salibián-Barrera
et al

[
31
]
.
For the multivariate linear regression model of Example
2
,
our MM-estimators
\bm{\beta}_{1,n}
and
\sigma_{n}^{2}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{n})
coincide
with the ones for multivariate linear regression in Kudraszow and Maronna
[
15
]
.
If, in addition
k=1
, our regression MM-estimator coincides with the one for multiple linear regression, as introduced in Yohai
[
36
]
.
Our MM-functionals then coincide with the M-functional with general scale
\sigma(P)
,
as treated in Martin
et al

[
25
]
.

4
Existence

We will first establish existence of the functionals
\sigma(P)
,
\bm{\beta}_{1}(P)
,
\bm{\gamma}(P)
, and
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)
,
under particular conditions on the probability measure
P
.
As a consequence, this will also yield the existence of the estimators
\sigma_{n}
,
\bm{\beta}_{1,n}
,
\bm{\gamma}_{n}
, and
\bm{\theta}_{1,n}
.
Recall that the observations are represented as points
(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
.
Note however, that for linear models with intercept the first column of each
\mathbf{X}_{i}
consists of 1’s.
This means that the points
(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})
are concentrated in a lower dimensional subset of
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
.
A similar situation occurs when all
\mathbf{X}_{i}
are equal to the same design matrix, such as in Copt and Heritier
[
4
]
.
In view of this, define
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{kq}
as the subset with the lowest dimension

p=\text{dim}(\mathcal{X})\leq kq
satisfying

P(\mathbf{X}\in\mathcal{X})=1.

(4.1)

Hence,
P
is then concentrated on the subset
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
of
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
, which
is of dimension
k+p
, which may be smaller than
k+kq
.

The first condition that we require, expresses the fact that
P

cannot have too much mass at infinity, in relation to the ratio
r_{0}=b_{0}/\sup\rho_{0}
.

(\mathrm{C1}_{\epsilon})

There exists a compact set
K_{\epsilon}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
,
such that
P(K_{\epsilon})\geq r_{0}+\epsilon
.

The second condition requires that
P
cannot have too much mass at arbitrarily thin strips in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
.
For
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{k+kq}
, such that
\|\bm{\alpha}\|=1
,
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\delta\geq 0
, we define a strip

H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,\delta)
as follows:

H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,\delta)=\left\{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}:\ell-\delta/2\leq\bm{\alpha}^{T}\mathbf{s}\leq\ell+\delta/2\right\}.

(4.2)

Defined in this way, a strip is the area between two parallel hyperplanes
which are symmetric around the hyperplane
H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,0)=\left\{\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}:\bm{\alpha}^{T}\mathbf{s}=\ell\right\}
.
Since the distance between two parallel hyperplanes

\bm{\alpha}^{T}\mathbf{s}=\ell_{1}
and
\bm{\alpha}^{T}\mathbf{s}=\ell_{2}
is
|\ell_{1}-\ell_{2}|
,
the strip
H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,\delta)
defined in (
4.2
) has width
\delta
.
We require the following condition.

(\mathrm{C2}_{\epsilon})

The value

\delta_{\epsilon}=\inf\left\{\delta:P\left(H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,\delta)\right)\geq\epsilon,\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{k+kq},\|\bm{\alpha}\|=1,\ell\in\mathbb{R},\delta\geq 0\right\}

is strictly positive.

According to (
4.1
), in
(\mathrm{C2}_{\epsilon})
one only needs to consider strips in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
.

Both conditions are satisfied for any
0<\epsilon\leq 1-r_{0}
by any probability measure
P

that is absolutely continuous.
Clearly, condition
(\mathrm{C1}_{\epsilon})
holds for any
0\leq\epsilon\leq 1-r_{0}
for
the empirical measure
\mathbb{P}_{n}
corresponding to a collection of
n
points

\mathcal{S}_{n}=\{\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
.
Condition
(\mathrm{C2}_{\epsilon})
with
\epsilon=(k+p+1)/n

is also satisfied by the empirical measure
\mathbb{P}_{n}
, when the collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
is in
general position
, i.e.,
no subset
J\subset\mathcal{S}_{n}
of
k+p+1
points is contained in the same hyperplane
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
.
Conditions
(\mathrm{C1}_{\epsilon})
and
(\mathrm{C2}_{\epsilon})
are the same as in
Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
and they
are similar to condition
(\mathrm{C}_{\epsilon})

in Lopuhaä
[
20
]
.
The reason that
(\mathrm{C1}_{\epsilon})
slightly deviates from Lopuhaä
[
20
]
, is to handle
the presence of
\mathbf{X}
in minimizing (
3.10
).

To establish existence of
\sigma(P)
we follow the reasoning in Yohai
[
36
]
.
We require the following condition.

(C0)

For

E_{0}=\left\{(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}:\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0}(P)\|=0\right\}
,
it holds
P(E_{0})<1-b_{0}/\sup\rho_{0}
.

We then have the following lemma.

Lemma 1
.

Let
\rho_{0}
satisfy (R1)-(R3) and let
(\bm{\beta}_{0}(P),\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\bm{\Theta}
be the pair of initial functionals at
P
,
such that (C0) holds.
Then a solution
\sigma(P)>0
to (
3.8
) exists and is unique.

To establish the existence of
(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}(P))
, we follow the reasoning in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
.
The idea is to argue that one can restrict oneself to a compact set for finding solutions to
minimizing
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
subject to
|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})|=1
.
When
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
is continuous, this immediately yields the existence of a minimum.
To this end, we assume the following condition.

(V1)

The mapping
\bm{\theta}\mapsto\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is continuous.

To restrict oneself to
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma})
in a compact set, we make use of Lemma 4.1
in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
.
It requires that the identity is an element of

\mathcal{V}=\{\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})\in\text{PDS}(k):\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}\subset\mathbb{R}^{l}\}

and that
\mathcal{V}
is closed under multiplication with a positive scalar.

(V2)

There exists a
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}\subset\mathbb{R}^{l}
, such that
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbf{I}_{k}
.
For any
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})\in\mathcal{V}
and any
\alpha>0
, it holds that

\alpha\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}^{\prime})
, for some
\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\in\bm{\Theta}\subset\mathbb{R}^{l}
.

Conditions (V1)-(V2) are not very restrictive.
For example, all examples discussed in Section
2

satisfy these conditions.
Also note that (V2) implies that (
3.11
) has a solution
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)

and similarly for (
3.6
).

Lemma 4.1 in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
will ensure that there exists a compact set
in
\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\text{PDS}(k)

that contains all pairs
(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
that correspond to possible minima

(\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma})
of
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
.
To establish that there also exists a compact set in
\mathfrak{D}
that contains all
possible minima
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma})
of
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
,
we need that the pre-image

\{\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}:\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})\in K\}
of a compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
is again compact.
Recall that subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{l}
are compact if and only if they are closed and bounded,
and note that the pre-image of a continuous mapping of a closed set is closed.
Hence, in view of condition (V1), it suffices to require the following condition.

(V3)

The mapping
\bm{\theta}\mapsto\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is such that the pre-image of a bounded set is bounded.

We then have the following theorem.

Theorem 1
.

Let
\rho_{0}
and
\rho_{1}
satisfy (R1)-(R2) and (
3.3
).
Suppose
\rho_{1}
is continuous and suppose that
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V1)-(V3).
Suppose
P
satisfies
(\text{C1}_{\epsilon})
and
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
, for some
0<\epsilon\leq 1-r_{0}
,
where
r_{0}=b_{0}/\sup\rho_{0}
.
Let
(\bm{\beta}_{0}(P),\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\bm{\Theta}
be the pair of initial functionals at
P

and let
\sigma(P)
be a solution to (
3.8
).
Then there exists a pair
(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}(P))\in\mathfrak{D}

that minimizes
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))

and a vector
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)\in\bm{\Theta}
that is the unique solution of (
3.11
).

Theorem
1
has a direct corollary for the existence of the MM-estimators, when dealing with a collections of points.
Let
\mathcal{S}_{n}=\{\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{n}\}
, with
\mathbf{s}_{i}=(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})
, be a collection of
n
points
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
.
Define

\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})=\text{maximal number of points of $\mathcal{S}_{n}$ lying on the same hyperplane in~$\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}$.}

(4.3)

For example, if the distribution
P
is absolutely continuous, then

\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})\leq k+p
with probability one.
Existence of
\sigma_{n}
can be obtained from Lemma
1
.
Suppose that (
3.12
) holds and
that
\#\{i:1\leq i\leq n,\,\|\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta}_{0,n}\|=0\}<n(1-b_{0}/\sup\rho_{0})
.
Then
\mathbb{P}_{n}
satisfies condition (C0), so that the solution
\sigma_{n}
of (
3.2
)
exists and is unique, according to Lemma
1
.
We then have the following corollary.

Corollary 1
.

Suppose that
\rho_{0}
,
\rho_{1}
, and
\mathbf{V}
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
1
.
For a collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}=\{\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
,
with
\mathbf{s}_{i}=(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
,
let
(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\theta}_{0,n})\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\mathbb{R}^{l}
be the pair of initial estimators
satisfying (
3.12
) and let
\sigma_{n}
be a solution to (
3.2
).
If
\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})+1\leq n(1-r_{0})
, where
r_{0}=b_{0}/\sup\rho_{0}
,
then there exists a pair
(\bm{\beta}_{1,n},\bm{\gamma}_{n})\in\mathfrak{D}
that minimizes
R_{n}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))

and a vector
\bm{\theta}_{1,n}
that is the unique solution of (
3.6
).

For the multivariate linear regression model of Example
2
,
Kudraszow and Maronna
[
15
]
prove existence of
\bm{\beta}_{1,n}=\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{B}_{1,n}^{T})

and
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{n})
, assuming
\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})<n/2
.
Hence, their Theorem 1 follows from our Corollary
1
,
as long as
r_{0}\leq 1/2-1/n
.
This holds for example, when S-estimators with maximal breakdown point are used as initial estimators
(see Theorem 6.1 in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
).
Existence of the corresponding functionals is not discussed in Kudraszow and Maronna
[
15
]
.
This now follows from our Theorem
1
.
For the multivariate location and scatter model in Example
3
,
the MM-functionals coincide with the multivariate location and scatter M-functionals defined
with loss function
\rho_{1}
and with auxiliary scale
\sigma(P)
,
defined as the solution of (
3.8
).
Tatsuoka and Tyler
[
32
]
establish existence for these functionals
under the assumption

\inf_{(\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma})\in\mathfrak{D}}R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))<(1-P(B))\sup\rho_{1},

(4.4)

for all hyperplanes
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
It can be seen, using (
3.2
) and (
3.3
), that if our condition
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
holds for some
\epsilon<1-r_{0}
,
then condition (
4.4
) is satisfied.

Existence of MM-estimators has been obtained from the existence of MM-functionals at the empirical measure
\mathbb{P}_{n}
,
which converges to
P
, as
n
tends to infinity.
The following corollary shows that existence can be established in general,
for probability measures that are close to
P
.
This will become useful when we want to establish existence at
perturbed measures
(1-h)P+h\delta_{\mathbf{s}}
, for
h
sufficiently small, in order to determine the influence function of the functionals at
P

(see Section
8
).
It requires the following condition on
P
.

(C3)

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be the class of all measurable convex subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
.
Every
C\in\mathfrak{C}
is a
P
-continuity set, i.e.,
P(\partial C)=0
,
where
\partial C
denotes the boundary of
C
.

Condition (C3) is needed to apply Theorem 4.2 in Ranga Rao
[
27
]
.
Clearly, this condition is satisfied if
P
is absolutely continuous.

Corollary 2
.

Suppose that
\rho_{0}
satisfies the conditions of Lemma
1
.
Let
P
satisfy (C0) and (C3), and let
(\bm{\beta}_{0}(P),\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\bm{\Theta}

be the pair of initial functionals at
P
.
Let
P_{t}
,
t\geq 0
, be a sequence of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
that converges weakly to
P
, as
t\to\infty
.
Suppose that
(\bm{\beta}_{0}(P_{t}),\bm{\theta}_{0}(P_{t}))
exist, for
t
sufficiently large,
such that
\bm{\beta}_{0}(P_{t})\to\bm{\beta}_{0}(P)
.
Then

(i)

for
t
sufficiently large,
equation (
3.8
) with
P=P_{t}
, has a unique solution
\sigma(P_{t})
.

In addition, suppose that
\rho_{0}
,
\rho_{1}
, and
\mathbf{V}
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
1
,
and suppose that
P
satisfies
(\text{C1}_{\epsilon^{\prime}})
and
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
,
for some
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{\prime}\leq 1-r_{0}
, where
r_{0}=b_{0}/\sup\rho_{0}
.
Then

(ii)

for
t
sufficiently large,
there exists
(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P_{t}),\bm{\gamma}(P_{t}))\in\mathfrak{D}
that minimizes
R_{P_{t}}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))

and a vector
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P_{t})\in\bm{\Theta}
that is the unique solution of equation (
3.11
) with
P=P_{t}
.

5
Continuity and consistency

Consider a sequence
P_{t}
,
t\geq 0
, of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
that converges weakly to
P
, as
t\to\infty
.
By continuity of the MM-functional
(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\theta}_{1}(P))
we mean that

(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P_{t}),\bm{\theta}_{1}(P_{t}))\to(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\theta}_{1}(P))
, as
t\to\infty
.
An example of such a sequence is the sequence of empirical measures
\mathbb{P}_{n}
,
n=1,2,\ldots
, that converges weakly to
P
, almost surely.
Continuity of the MM-functional for this sequence would then mean that the MM-estimator

(\bm{\beta}_{1,n},\bm{\theta}_{1,n})
is consistent,
i.e.,
(\bm{\beta}_{1}(\mathbb{P}_{n}),\bm{\theta}_{1}(\mathbb{P}_{n}))\to(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\theta}_{1}(P))
,
almost surely.

We have the following theorem establishing continuity of the MM-functionals.

Theorem 2
.

Let
\rho_{0}
satisfy (R2)-(R3) and
\mathbf{V}
satisfy (V1).
Let
(\bm{\beta}_{0}(P),\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\bm{\Theta}
be the pair of initial functionals at
P
.
Let
P_{t}
,
t\geq 0
, be a sequence of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
that converges weakly to
P
, as
t\to\infty
.
Suppose that
(\bm{\beta}_{0}(P_{t}),\bm{\theta}_{0}(P_{t}))
exist, for
t
sufficiently large,
and suppose that
(\bm{\beta}_{0}(P_{t}),\bm{\theta}_{0}(P_{t}))\to(\bm{\beta}_{0}(P),\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))
.
Let
\sigma(P)
be the unique solution of (
3.8
)
and let
\sigma(P_{t})
be a solution of (
3.8
), with
P=P_{t}
.
Then

(i)

\sigma(P_{t})\to\sigma(P)
, as
t\to\infty
.

In addition, suppose that
\rho_{1}
satisfies (
3.3
)
and (R2)-(R3),
and that
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V3).
Suppose that
P
satisfies (C3), as well as
(\text{C1}_{\epsilon^{\prime}})
and
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
,
for some
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{\prime}\leq 1-r_{0}
,
where
r_{0}=b_{0}/\sup\rho_{0}
.
For
t
sufficiently large,
let
(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P_{t}),\bm{\gamma}(P_{t}))\in\mathfrak{D}

be a local minimum of
R_{P_{t}}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
that satisfies (
3.10
) for
P=P_{t}
,
and let
(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}(P))\in\mathfrak{D}
be the unique minimizer of
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
.
Then

(ii)

(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P_{t}),\bm{\gamma}(P_{t}))\to(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}(P))
, as
t\to\infty
;

Let
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)
and
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P_{t})
be solutions of (
3.11
)
and (
3.11
) with
P=P_{t}
, respectively.
Then

(iii)

\bm{\theta}_{1}(P_{t})\to\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)
, as
t\to\infty
.

Continuity of the MM-functionals will be used to derive the influence function of the MM-functionals in Section
8
.
Another convenient consequence of the continuity of the MM-functionals is that one can directly obtain consistency of the MM-estimators.
Let
\mathcal{S}_{n}=\{\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{n}\}
, with
\mathbf{s}_{i}=(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})
, be a collection of
n
points
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
.
We apply Theorem
2
to the sequence
\mathbb{P}_{n}
,
n=1,2,\ldots
,
of probability measures, where
\mathbb{P}_{n}
is the empirical measure corresponding to
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.

Corollary 3
.

Let
\rho_{0}
and
\mathbf{V}
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
2
(i).
For a collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}=\{\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
,
with
\mathbf{s}_{i}=(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
,
let
(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\theta}_{0,n})\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\mathbb{R}^{l}
be the pair of initial estimators
satisfying (
3.12
)
and suppose that
(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\theta}_{0,n})\to(\bm{\beta}_{0}(P),\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))
, with probability one.
Let
\sigma(P)
the unique solution of (
3.8
)
and let
\sigma_{n}
be a solution of (
3.2
).
Then

(i)

\sigma_{n}\to\sigma(P)
, with probability one.

In addition, suppose
\rho_{1}
,
\mathbf{V}
, and
P
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
2
(ii).
Let
(\bm{\beta}_{1,n},\bm{\gamma}_{n})\in\mathfrak{D}
be a local minimum of
R_{n}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))

that satisfies (
3.5
),
and let
(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}(P))\in\mathfrak{D}
be the unique minimizer of
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
.
Then

(ii)

(\bm{\beta}_{1,n},\bm{\gamma}_{n})\to(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}(P))
, with probability one;

Let
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)
and
\bm{\theta}_{1,n}
be solutions of (
3.11
)
and (
3.6
), respectively.
Then

(iii)

\bm{\theta}_{1,n}\to\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)
, with probability one.

When
\mathbf{V}
also satisfies (V1), then for the covariance MM-estimator
it follows from Corollary
3
that

\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1,n})\to\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1}(P))=\sigma^{2}(P)\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P))
,
with probability one.
This extends Theorem 5 in Kudraszow and Maronna
[
15
]
.
Their result applies to MM-estimators for the multivariate models
in Examples
2
and
3
,
but is obtained only for distributions with an elliptical contoured density.

Theorem
2
and Corollary
3
require that
(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}(P))\in\mathfrak{D}

uniquely minimizes
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
.
This situation is very similar to that of multivariate location-scatter M-estimators with
auxiliary scale, considered by Tatsuoka and Tyler
[
32
]
.
For the special case that
\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{I}_{k}
, their Theorem 4.2 shows that
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{C})
has a unique minimum
for a broad class of distributions,
consisting of affine transformations of distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
which are invariant under permutations and sign changes of its components and which have densities
g
such that

g\circ\exp
is Schur-concave (see
[
32
]
for details), i.e.,

f_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\mathbf{y})=|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{-1/2}g(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}(\mathbf{y}-\bm{\mu})).

(5.1)

The next theorem is a direct consequence of that result.
Note that elliptically contoured densities
are special cases of (
5.1
).
Let
\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}
denote the expectation with respect to
f_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}
.

Theorem 3
.

Let
\rho_{0}
satisfy (R1)-(R3) and suppose
\rho_{1}
is continuous and
satisfies (R2) and (
3.3
).
Suppose that
P
is absolutely continuous,
such that for some
(\bm{\beta}^{*},\bm{\theta}^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\bm{\Theta}
,
for all
\mathbf{X}
, the distribution of
\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{X}
has density
f_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}
from (
5.1
), with
\bm{\mu}=\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}^{*}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}^{*})
.
Suppose that
g
in (
5.1
)
is strictly
M
-concave (see
[
32
, Definition 4.4]
).
Suppose
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V1)-(V3) and suppose
\mathbf{X}
has full rank with probability one.
Let
(\bm{\beta}_{0}(P),\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\bm{\Theta}
be the pair of initial functionals at
P

satisfying
(\bm{\beta}_{0}(P),\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))=(\bm{\beta}^{*},\bm{\theta}^{*})
.
Then, the following holds with probability one.

(i)

Equation (
3.8
) has a unique solution
\sigma(P)

and the function

R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
has a unique minimum
(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}(P))\in\mathfrak{D}
,
that satisfies

\bm{\beta}_{1}(P)=\bm{\beta}^{*}
and

\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P))=\mathbf{\Sigma}/|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/k}
.

(ii)

When
\mathbf{V}(\alpha\bm{\theta})=\alpha\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
, for all
\alpha>0
, then
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)=\bm{\theta}^{*}\sigma^{2}(P)/|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/k}
.

(iii)

When
b_{0}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\rho_{0}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)
, then
\sigma(P)=|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/(2k)}
.

An example of initial functionals
(\bm{\beta}_{0}(P),\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))
that satisfy the
conditions of Theorem
3
, are the S-functionals defined with loss function
\rho_{0}
,
see Theorem 5.3 in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]

or Theorem 1 in Davies
[
7
]
for the multivariate location-scatter model.

The proof of Theorem
3
depends heavily on the application of
Theorem 4.2 in Tatsuoka and Tyler
[
32
]
on the uniqueness
of multivariate M-functionals with auxiliary scale.
It considers strict M-concave densities
g
in (
5.1
),
which is a broad class of densities that includes spherical symmetric densities,
among others, see Tatsuoka and Tyler
[
32
]
for details.
In this way, Theorem
3
can be seen as an extension
of Theorem 1 in Davies
[
7
]
on the uniqueness of multivariate
location-scatter S-functionals at distributions with an elliptically contoured density.

6
Global robustness: the breakdown point

Consider a collection of points
\mathcal{S}_{n}=\{\mathbf{s}_{i}=(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i}),i=1,\ldots,n\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
.
To investigate the global robustness of the estimators,
we compute their finite-sample (replacement) breakdown point.
For a given collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
, the finite-sample breakdown point
(see Donoho and Huber
[
9
]
)
of an estimator is defined as the smallest proportion of points
from
\mathcal{S}_{n}
that one needs to replace in order to
send the estimator to the boundary of its parameter space.
To emphasize the dependence on the collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
,
denote an estimator for the regression parameter by
\bm{\beta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{n})

and an estimator for the vector of covariance parameters by
\bm{\theta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{n})
.
For a given collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
, the finite-sample breakdown point
of a regression estimator
\bm{\beta}_{n}
is defined as

\epsilon_{n}^{*}(\bm{\beta}_{n},\mathcal{S}_{n})=\min_{1\leq m\leq n}\left\{\frac{m}{n}:\sup_{\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\left\|\bm{\beta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{n})-\bm{\beta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime})\right\|=\infty\right\},

(6.1)

where the minimum runs over all possible collections
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
that can be obtained from
\mathcal{S}_{n}

by replacing
m
points of
\mathcal{S}_{n}
by arbitrary points in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
.

An estimator
\bm{\theta}_{n}
for the vector of covariance parameters determines the covariance estimator
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{n})
.
For this reason it seems natural to let the breakdown point of
\bm{\theta}_{n}
correspond to the breakdown of a covariance estimator.
For any
k\times k
matrix
\mathbf{A}
, let
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{A})\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A})
denote the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{A}
.
We define the finite sample (replacement) breakdown point of an estimator
\bm{\theta}_{n}
at a collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
, as

\epsilon_{n}^{*}(\bm{\theta}_{n},\mathcal{S}_{n})=\min_{1\leq m\leq n}\left\{\frac{m}{n}:\sup_{\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\text{dist}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{n}))),\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}))=\infty\right\},

(6.2)

with
\text{dist}(\cdot,\cdot)
defined as

\text{dist}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})=\max\left\{\left|\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A})-\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{B})\right|,\left|\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{A})^{-1}-\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{B})^{-1}\right|\right\}
,
where the minimum runs over all possible collections
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
that can be obtained from
\mathcal{S}_{n}

by replacing
m
points of
\mathcal{S}_{n}
by arbitrary points in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
.
So the breakdown point of
\bm{\theta}_{n}
is the smallest proportion of points from
\mathcal{S}_{n}
that one needs to replace in order to
make the largest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}))
arbitrarily large (explosion), or
to make the smallest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}))
arbitrarily small (implosion).

Good global robustness is illustrated by a high breakdown point.
The breakdown point of the MM-estimators is given in the theorem below.

Theorem 4
.

Let
\rho_{0}
satisfy (R1)-(R3).
Let
\rho_{1}
satisfy (R2) and (
3.3
)
and suppose
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V1)-(V3).
Let
\mathcal{S}_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
be a collection of
n
points
\mathbf{s}_{i}=(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})
,
i=1,\ldots,n
.
Let
r_{0}=b_{0}/\sup\rho_{0}
and suppose that
0<\lfloor nr_{0}\rfloor<n-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})
,
where
\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})
is defined by (
4.3
).
Let
(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\theta}_{0,n})
be initial estimators for
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta})
.
Let
(\bm{\beta}_{1,n},\bm{\gamma}_{n})\in\mathfrak{D}
satisfy (
3.5
)
and let
\bm{\theta}_{1,n}
be a solution of (
3.6
).
Then

\epsilon^{*}_{n}(\bm{\beta}_{1,n},\bm{\gamma}_{n},\bm{\theta}_{1,n},\mathcal{S}_{n})\geq\min\left\{\epsilon^{*}_{n}(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\theta}_{0,n},\mathcal{S}_{n}),\frac{\lceil nr_{0}\rceil}{n},\frac{\lceil n-nr_{0}\rceil-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})}{n}\right\}.

An example of initial estimators
(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\theta}_{0,n})
with high breakdown point,
are S-estimators defined with the function
\rho_{0}
,
as discussed in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
.
According to their Theorem 6.1 and Remark 3,
it holds that

\epsilon^{*}_{n}(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\theta}_{0,n},\mathcal{S}_{n})\geq\min\{\lceil nr_{0}\rceil,\lceil n-nr_{0}\rceil-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})\}/n
.
In this case, the lower bound in Theorem
4
simplifies to

\min\{\lceil nr_{0}\rceil,\lceil n-nr_{0}\rceil-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})\}/n
.
The largest possible value of this lower bound is attained when
r_{0}=(n-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n}))/(2n)
.
In this case

\lceil nr_{0}\rceil=\lceil n-nr_{0}\rceil-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})=\lceil(n-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n}))/2\rceil=\lfloor(n-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})+1)/2\rfloor
.
When the collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
is in general position, then
\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})=k+p
.
In that case the breakdown point of the MM-estimators is at least equal
to
\lfloor(n-k-p+1)/2\rfloor/n
.
When all
\mathbf{X}_{i}
are equal to the same
\mathbf{X}
,
as in the multivariate location-scatter model, but
also in the linear mixed effects models considered in Copt and Victoria-Feser
[
5
]

and Copt and Heritier
[
4
]
,
one has
p=0
and
\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})=k
.
In that case, the lower bound of the breakdown point is equal to
\lfloor(n-k+1)/2\rfloor/n
.
This value coincides with the maximal breakdown point for affine equivariant estimators
for
k\times k
covariance matrices (see Davies
[
7
, Theorem 6]
).

The breakdown point for (a simpler version of) regression MM-estimators for the
linear mixed effects model (
2.3
)
has only been discussed in Copt and Heritier
[
4
]
.
They conjecture that the exact value can be derived using the technique in
Van Aelst and Willems
[
35
]
, but do not pursue a rigorous derivation.
The result in Theorem
4
applies to the current more extensive version of MM-estimators
for the linear mixed effects model (
2.3
).
Furthermore, for
0<r_{0}\leq(n-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n}))/(2n)
, it holds that

\lceil nr_{0}\rceil\leq\lceil n-nr_{0}\rceil-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})
.
In this case, the lower bound for the breakdown point in Theorem
4

coincides with that of the regression MM-estimator considered in Lopuhaä
[
18
]
.

For the multivariate linear regression model,
Kudraszow and Maronna
[
15
]
take
r_{0}=1/2
and consider the case
\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})<n/2
.
For this situation
\lceil nr_{0}\rceil>\lceil n-nr_{0}\rceil-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})
.
Hence, their Theorem 3 follows from our Theorem
4
for the case
r_{0}=1/2
.
For the multivariate location-scatter model, Salibián-Barrera
et al

[
31
]

consider MM-estimators with S-estimators as initial estimators.
Our Theorem
4
then coincides with their Theorem 1.
For the MM-estimators in this model, Tyler
[
34
]
considers the gross error breakdown point,
which for finite collections is related to the finite sample contamination breakdown point.

7
Score equations

Up to this point, properties of MM-functionals and MM-estimators have been derived from
minimizing
R_{n}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
and
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
,
as defined in (
3.4
) and (
3.9
), respectively.
To obtain the influence function and to establish the limiting distribution of MM-estimators,
we use the score equations that can be found by differentiation of the Lagrangian corresponding to the
constrained minimization problem.
To this end, we require the following additional condition on the function
\rho_{1}
,

(R4)

\rho_{1}
is continuously differentiable and
u_{1}(s)=\rho_{1}^{\prime}(s)/s
is continuous,

and the following condition on the mapping
\bm{\theta}\mapsto\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
,

(V4)

\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is continuously differentiable.

Obviously, condition (V4) implies the former condition (V1).
For

\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
and
\mathbf{C}\in\text{PDS}(k)
, define the Mahalanobis distances by

d^{2}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{t},\mathbf{C})=(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{t})^{T}\mathbf{C}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{t}).

(7.1)

We then have the following proposition.

Proposition 1
.

Let
\rho_{1}
satisfy (R2) and (R4), and
\mathbf{V}
satisfy (V4), and suppose that
\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}\|<\infty
.
Let
(\bm{\beta}_{0}(P),\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))
be the pair of initial functionals and let
\sigma(P)
be a solution of (
3.8
).
Then any local minimum
\bm{\xi}(P)=(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}(P))\in\mathfrak{D}
of
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
satisfies

\int\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi},\sigma(P))\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})=\mathbf{0},

(7.2)

where
\Psi=(\Psi_{\bm{\beta}},\Psi_{\bm{\gamma}})
, with
\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}
and
\Psi_{\bm{\gamma}}=(\Psi_{\bm{\gamma},1},\ldots,\Psi_{\bm{\gamma},l})

given by

\begin{split}\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi},\sigma)&=u_{1}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma}\right)\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\\
\Psi_{\bm{\gamma},j}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi},\sigma)&=u_{1}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma}\right)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{1,j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad-\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{j}}\right)\log|\mathbf{V}|,\end{split}

(7.3)

with
d=d(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
, as defined in (
7.1
),
and
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})
is abbreviated by
\mathbf{V}
, and where

\mathbf{H}_{1,j}=\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{j}}\right)\left(\sum_{t=1}^{l}\gamma_{t}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{t}}\right)-\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{l}\gamma_{t}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{t}}\right)\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{j}},

(7.4)

for
j=1,\ldots,l
.

Since
d(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))/\sigma=d(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta},\sigma^{2}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
,
the regression score equation for
\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}
is similar to
the one for the regression MM-estimator considered in Lopuhaä
[
18
]
,
defined with initial covariance functional
\mathbf{V}_{0}(P)
,
and both score equations coincide when
\mathbf{V}_{0}(P)=\sigma^{2}(P)\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P))
.
Similarly, the regression score equation for
\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}
with the empirical measure
\mathbb{P}_{n}
for
P
in (
7.2
)
is similar to equation (8) for the regression MM-estimator in the linear mixed effects model considered in Copt and Heritier
[
4
]
,
defined with initial covariance estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{S}
,
and both equations coincide when
\widehat{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{S}=\sigma_{n}^{2}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{n})
.
For the multivariate linear regression model, the empirical score equation for

\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}
coincides with equation (2.10) for the regression MM-estimator
discussed in Kudraszow and Maronna
[
15
]
.
When the initial estimators
(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\theta}_{0,n})
are S-estimators, then

\sigma_{n}=|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{0,n})|^{1/(2k)}
.
In that case, for the multivariate location-scatter model,
the empirical score equation for
\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}

coincides with fixed point equation (16) for the location MM-estimator
considered in Salibián-Barrera
et al

[
31
]
.

The function
\Psi_{\bm{\gamma}}
simplifies in situations where
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})
has a linear structure, that is

\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})=\sum_{j=1}^{l}\gamma_{j}\mathbf{L}_{j},

(7.5)

The covariance structures in Examples
1
,
2
, and
3
,
satisfy this property.

Proposition 2
.

Suppose the conditions of Proposition
1
hold and that
\mathbf{V}

has a linear structure (
7.5
).
Let
v_{1}(s)=\rho_{1}^{\prime}(s)s
and let
\mathbf{L}
be the
k^{2}\times l
matrix

\mathbf{L}=\Big[\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathrm{vec}\left(\mathbf{L}_{1}\right)&\cdots&\mathrm{vec}\left(\mathbf{L}_{l}\right)\\
\end{array}\Big].

(7.6)

Then any local minimum

\bm{\xi}(P)=(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}(P))\in\mathfrak{D}
of
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))

satisfies (
7.2
),
with
\Psi=(\Psi_{\bm{\beta}},\Psi_{\bm{\gamma}})
, where

\begin{split}\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi},\sigma)&=u_{1}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma}\right)\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\\
\Psi_{\bm{\gamma}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi},\sigma)&=-\mathbf{L}^{T}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right)\mathrm{vec}\left(\Psi_{\mathbf{V}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi},\sigma)\right),\end{split}

(7.7)

where

\Psi_{\mathbf{V}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi},\sigma)=ku_{1}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma}\right)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}-v_{1}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma}\right)\sigma^{2}\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{V}\log|\mathbf{V}|,

(7.8)

with
d=d(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
, as defined in (
7.1
),
and
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})
is abbreviated by
\mathbf{V}
.

For the multivariate linear regression model in Example
2
,
one has
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})=\bm{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Sigma}/|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/k}
, with
\bm{\gamma}=\mathrm{vech}(\bm{\Gamma})
.
The matrix
\mathbf{L}=\partial\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V})/\partial\bm{\gamma}^{T}
is then equal to the so-called duplication matrix
\mathcal{D}_{k}
,
which is the unique
k^{2}\times k(k+1)/2
matrix, with the properties

\mathcal{D}_{k}\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{C})=\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{C})
and
(\mathcal{D}_{k}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{k})^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{k}^{T}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{C})=\mathrm{vech}(\mathbf{C})

(e.g., see
[
24
, Ch. 3, Sec. 8]
).
Because
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})
has full rank, it follows that equation (
7.2
) holds for
\Psi=(\Psi_{\bm{\beta}},\Psi_{\mathbf{V}})
.
When we insert
\sigma_{n}^{2}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{n})=\sigma_{n}^{2}\bm{\Gamma}_{n}
, the resulting score equations for the empirical measure
\mathbb{P}_{n}
corresponding to observations

(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
, are then equivalent to
equations (2.10) and (2.11) found in
Kudraszow and Maronna
[
15
]
for the regression MM-estimators.
For the multivariate location-scatter model in Example
3
,
one also has
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})=\bm{\Gamma}
, with

\bm{\gamma}=\mathrm{vech}(\bm{\Gamma})
,
so that again equation (
7.2
) holds for
\Psi=(\Psi_{\bm{\beta}},\Psi_{\mathbf{V}})
.
When the initial estimators
(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\theta}_{0,n})
are S-estimators,
it can be shown that the empirical score equation for
\Psi_{\mathbf{V}}

coincides with fixed point equation (17) for the covariance shape MM-estimator
considered in Salibián-Barrera
et al

[
31
]
.

8
Local robustness: the influence function

For
0<h<1
and
\mathbf{s}=(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
fixed, define the perturbed probability measure

P_{h,\mathbf{s}}=(1-h)P+h\delta_{\mathbf{s}}
,
where
\delta_{\mathbf{s}}
denotes the Dirac measure at
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
.
The
influence function
of a functional
T(\cdot)
at probability measure
P
,
is defined as

\text{IF}(\mathbf{s};T,P)=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\frac{T((1-h)P+h\delta_{\mathbf{s}})-T(P)}{h},

(8.1)

if this limit exists.
In contrast to the global robustness measured by the breakdown point,
the influence function measures the local robustness.
It describes the effect of an infinitesimal contamination at a single point
\mathbf{s}
on the functional
(see Hampel
[
11
]
).
Good local robustness is therefore illustrated by a bounded influence function.

8.1
The general case

We will investigate when the limit in (
8.1
) exists for the functionals
\sigma
,

\bm{\xi}=(\bm{\beta}_{1},\bm{\gamma})
, and
\bm{\theta}_{1}

and derive their expression at general
P
.
Since the value of
\bm{\theta}_{1}
determines the covariance matrix
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1})
,
we also include the influence function of the covariance functional.
Consider the MM-functional at
P_{h,\mathbf{s}}
.
From the Portmanteau theorem
[
2
, Theorem 2.1]
it can easily be seen that
P_{h,\mathbf{s}}\to P
, weakly, as
h\downarrow 0
.
Therefore, under the conditions of Corollary
2
and Theorem
2
, it follows
that there exist a unique solution
\sigma(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})

of equation (
3.8
) with
P=P_{h,\mathbf{s}}
,
a pair
\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})=(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}}),\bm{\gamma}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}}))

that minimizes
R_{P_{h,\mathbf{s}}}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
over
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma})\in\mathfrak{D}
,
and a vector
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})\in\bm{\Theta}
that uniquely solves (
3.10
), for
P=P_{h,\mathbf{s}}
.
Moreover, under these conditions

(\sigma(P_{h,\mathbf{s}}),\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}}),\bm{\theta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}}))\to(\sigma(P),\bm{\xi}(P),\bm{\theta}_{1}(P))
,
as
h\downarrow 0
.

For
\bm{\xi}=(\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma})\in\mathfrak{D}
and
\sigma>0
,
define

\Lambda(\bm{\xi},\sigma)=\int\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi},\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s}),

(8.2)

where
\Psi=(\Psi_{\bm{\beta}},\Psi_{\bm{\gamma}})
is defined in (
7.3
),
and write
\Lambda=(\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}},\Lambda_{\bm{\gamma}})
, where

\begin{split}\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\xi},\sigma)&=\int\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi},\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s}),\qquad\Lambda_{\bm{\gamma}}(\bm{\xi},\sigma)=\int\Psi_{\bm{\gamma}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi},\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s}).\end{split}

(8.3)

By definition of the variance components MM-functional
\bm{\theta}_{1}
,
it can be expected that for general
P
,
the influence function of
\bm{\theta}_{1}
depends
on the influence functions of
\sigma
and
\bm{\gamma}
.
Furthermore, because the function
\Psi
depends on
\sigma
, it can be expected that for general
P
,
the influence function of
\bm{\xi}=(\bm{\beta}_{1},\bm{\gamma})
depends on the influence function of
\sigma
,
see Lemma
2
in the supplemental material
[
19
]
.
In turn, since the functional
\sigma
is defined as a solution of (
3.8
), it can be expected that for general
P
,
its influence function will depend on the initial functionals
\bm{\zeta}_{0}=(\bm{\beta}_{0},\bm{\theta}_{0})
,
see Lemma
3
in the supplemental material
[
19
]
.

The situation becomes a bit simpler if we assume some kind of symmetry of the distribution
P
.
A vector
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
is called a
point of symmetry
of
P
, if for almost all
\mathbf{X}
, it holds that

P\left(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{b}+A\mid\mathbf{X}\right)=P\left(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{b}-A\mid\mathbf{X}\right)
,
for all measurable sets
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
where for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
,

\mathbf{X}\mathbf{b}+\lambda A
denotes the set
\{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{b}+\lambda\mathbf{y}:\mathbf{y}\in A\}
.
If
\mathbf{b}
is a point of symmetry of
P
, it has the property that

\mathbb{E}[G(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\mathbf{b})]=\mathbf{0}
,
for any
G(\mathbf{z})
, which is an odd function of
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
Furthermore, in order to obtain simpler expressions we also require the following condition on the function
\rho_{1}
.

(R5)

\rho_{1}
is twice continuously differentiable.

Condition (R5) is needed to ensure
that
\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}/\partial\bm{\beta}
is continuous at
(\bm{\xi}(P),\sigma(P))
.
The expressions for
\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s};\bm{\gamma},P)
and
\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s};\sigma,P)

given in Lemmas
2
and
3
in
[
19
]

do simplify, but without further knowledge on the influence function
of
\bm{\zeta}_{0}
we can still not provide an explicit expression.
The situation is different for
\bm{\beta}_{1}
,
for which the influence function is given in the next theorem.

Theorem 5
.

Suppose
\rho_{1}
satisfies (R4) and
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V1).
Let
\sigma(P)
be a solution of (
3.8
) and let

\bm{\xi}(P)\in\mathfrak{D}
be a local minimum of
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
.
Let
\sigma(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})
be a solution of (
3.8
) with
P=P_{h,\mathbf{s}}

and let
\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})\in\mathfrak{D}
be a local minimum of
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
with
P=P_{h,\mathbf{s}}
.
Suppose that
(\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}}),\sigma(P_{h,\mathbf{s}}))\to(\bm{\xi}(P),\sigma(P))
,
as
h\downarrow 0
.
Let
\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}
be defined by (
8.3
)
with
\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}
from (
7.3
)
and suppose
\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}
is continuously differentiable
with a non-singular derivative

\mathbf{D}_{\bm{\beta}}=\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}/\partial\bm{\beta}
at
(\bm{\xi}(P),\sigma(P))
.
Moreover, suppose that
\bm{\beta}_{1}(P)
is a point of symmetry of
P
.
Then, for
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
, we have
\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s};\bm{\beta}_{1},P)=-\mathbf{D}_{\bm{\beta}}^{-1}\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P),\sigma(P))
.

The expression of
\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s};\bm{\beta}_{1},P)
in Theorem
5

is similar to that of the influence function of the regression MM-functional considered in Lopuhaä
[
18
]

defined with
\rho_{1}
and initial covariance functional
\mathbf{V}_{0}
, and both expressions
coincide when
\mathbf{V}_{0}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})\to\sigma^{2}(P)\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P))
.

If one is allowed to interchange integration and differentiation in
\mathbf{D}_{\bm{\beta}}
, then
the expression for
\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0};\bm{\beta}_{1},P)
in Corollary
5

coincides with that of the regression MM-functional in the multiple linear regression model considered in
Yohai
[
36
]
.
For linear mixed effects models, multivariate linear regression models, or multivariate location-scatter model,
expressions for the influence function of MM-functionals are either not available
or are restricted to distributions with an elliptically contoured density.
This situation is discussed in the next section for model (
2.1
).

8.2
Elliptically contoured densities

We can obtain an even more detailed expression for the influence functions,
when
\mathbf{V}
has a linear structure and
P
has the following property.

(E)

There exists
(\bm{\beta}^{*},\bm{\theta}^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\bm{\Theta}
,
such that for all
\mathbf{X}
, the distribution of
\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{X}
has an elliptically contoured density

f_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\mathbf{y})=|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{-1/2}m\left((\mathbf{y}-\bm{\mu})^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\bm{\mu})\right),

(8.4)

with
\bm{\mu}=\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}^{*}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}^{*})
and
m:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
.

We require the following condition on the mapping
\bm{\theta}\mapsto\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
.

(V5)

\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})
is twice continuously differentiable.

Condition (V5) is needed to
interchange the order of integration and differentiation in
\partial\Lambda/\partial\bm{\xi}
,
where
\Lambda
is defined in (
8.2
).
Clearly, condition (V5) implies former conditions (V4) and (V1).

When the MM-functionals are affine equivariant,
then it suffices to determine the influence function for the case
(\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma})=(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k})
.
However, this does not hold in general for the MM-functionals in our setting.
Nevertheless, for the general case with
\bm{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}\in\text{PDS}(k)
,
we can still use the fact that, conditionally on
\mathbf{X}
, the distribution of
\mathbf{y}
is the same as that of

\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1/2}\mathbf{z}+\bm{\mu}
, where
\mathbf{z}
has a spherical density
f_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}
.
Let

\begin{split}\alpha_{1}&=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\left(1-\frac{1}{k}\right)\frac{\rho_{1}^{\prime}\left(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|\right)}{c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|}+\frac{1}{k}\rho_{1}^{\prime\prime}\left(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|\right)\right],\\
\gamma_{1}&=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{0,\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\rho_{1}^{\prime\prime}(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|)(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|)^{2}+(k+1)\rho_{1}^{\prime}(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|)c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|\right]}{k+2},\end{split}

(8.5)

where
c_{\sigma}=|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/(2k)}/\sigma(P)
.
The influence functions of the MM-functionals
\bm{\beta}_{1}

and
\bm{\gamma}
at distributions
P
, such that
\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{X}
has an elliptical contoured density,
are now given by the following theorem.

Theorem 6
.

Suppose
P
satisfies (E) for some
(\bm{\beta}^{*},\bm{\theta}^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\bm{\Theta}

and
\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}\|^{2}<\infty
.
Let
\rho_{1}
satisfy (R2), (R4)-(R5)
and suppose
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V5) and has a linear structure (
7.5
).
Let
\sigma(P)
be a solution of (
3.8
) and let

\bm{\xi}(P)\in\mathfrak{D}
be a local minimum of
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))

that satisfies
\bm{\beta}_{1}(P)=\bm{\beta}^{*}
and
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P))=\mathbf{\Sigma}/|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/k}
.
Let
\sigma(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})
be a solution of (
3.8
) with
P=P_{h,\mathbf{s}}

and let
\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})\in\mathfrak{D}
be a local minimum of
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
with
P=P_{h,\mathbf{s}}
.
Suppose that
(\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}}),\sigma(P_{h,\mathbf{s}}))\to(\bm{\xi}(P),\sigma(P))
,
as
h\downarrow 0
.
Let
\alpha_{1}
be defined in (
8.5
) and suppose that
\alpha_{1}\neq 0
.
Suppose that
\mathbf{X}
has full rank with probability one.
Then, for
\mathbf{s}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
,

\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0};\bm{\beta}_{1},P)=\frac{u_{1}\left(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}_{0}\|\right)}{\alpha_{1}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\right]\right)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{0}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\mathbf{z}_{0},

where
u_{1}(s)=\rho_{1}^{\prime}(s)/s
,

\mathbf{z}_{0}=\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}(\mathbf{y}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}^{*})
, and
c_{\sigma}=|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/(2k)}/\sigma(P)
.
In addition,
suppose that
\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s};\sigma,P)
exists.
Let
\gamma_{1}
be defined in (
8.5
) and suppose that
\gamma_{1}>0
.
Suppose the
k^{2}\times l
matrix
\mathbf{L}
, as defined in (
7.6
), has full rank.
Then, for
\mathbf{s}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
,

\begin{split}\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0};\bm{\gamma},P)&=\frac{ku_{1}\left(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}_{0}\|\right)}{\sigma^{2}(P)\gamma_{1}}\Big(\mathbf{L}^{T}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1})\mathbf{L}\Big)^{-1}\mathbf{L}^{T}\left(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\right)\mathrm{vec}\left(\mathbf{z}_{0}\mathbf{z}_{0}^{T}\right)\\
&\quad-\frac{v_{1}\left(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}_{0}\|\right)}{|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/k}\gamma_{1}}\bm{\theta}^{*},\end{split}

where
v_{1}(s)=\rho_{1}^{\prime}(s)s
.

When
c_{\sigma}=1
(e.g., see Theorem
3
(iii)),
then
\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0};\bm{\beta}_{1},P)
coincides with the influence function
of the regression MM-functional considered in Lopuhaä
[
18
]

defined with
\rho_{1}
and an initial Fisher consistent covariance functional
\mathbf{V}_{0}
.
Moreover, it also coincides with the influence function of the regression S-functional defined with
\rho_{1}

for model (
2.1
),
see Corollary 8.4 in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
.
This confirms the claim made by Salibián-Barrera
et al

[
31
]

about the influence function of the location MM-functional in the model
of Example
3
.

For the linear mixed effects model (
2.3
),
Copt and Heritier
[
4
]
discuss the influence function, but an expression is not provided.
The expression for the influence function of the regression MM-functional now follows from
Theorem
6
.
For the multivariate linear regression model of Example
2
,
the expression for
\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0};\bm{\beta}_{1},P)
in Theorem
6

coincides with one found for the regression MM-functional in Kudraszow and Maronna
[
15
]
.
Since the multivariate location-scatter model of Example
3

is a special case of the multivariate linear regression model
by taking
\mathbf{x}=1
and
\mathbf{B}^{T}=\bm{\mu}
,
this also yields the expression for the influence function of the location MM-functional.
Finally, there is an interesting connection with the CM-functionals considered in Kent and Tyler
[
14
]
,
whose influence function depends on a parameter
\lambda_{0}
.
The expression for
\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0};\bm{\beta}_{1},P)
in Theorem
6
is similar
to the one for the location CM-functional and they both coincide when
c_{\sigma}=1/\sqrt{\lambda_{0}}
.

It remains to determine the influence function of the variance component MM-functional
\bm{\theta}_{1}
.
This is done in the next corollary.

Corollary 4
.

Suppose that the conditions hold of Theorem
6

and suppose that
\rho_{0}
satisfies (R2) and (R4).
Let
\bm{\zeta}_{0}=(\bm{\beta}_{0},\bm{\theta}_{0})
be the pair of initial functionals
satisfying
(\bm{\beta}_{0}(P),\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))=(\bm{\beta}^{*},\bm{\theta}^{*})
,
and suppose that
\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\zeta}_{0},P)
exists.
Let
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)
and
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}})
be solutions of equation (
3.11
)
and equation (
3.11
) with
P=P_{h,\mathbf{s}_{0}}
, respectively.
Let
u_{1}(s)=\rho_{1}^{\prime}(s)/s
and
v_{1}(s)=\rho_{1}^{\prime}(s)s
,
and let
\mathbf{L}
be defined in (
7.6
).
Let
c_{\sigma}=|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/(2k)}/\sigma(P)
and suppose that

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}[\rho_{0}^{\prime}\left(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|\right)c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|]>0
.
Then, for
\mathbf{s}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
,

\begin{split}\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\theta}_{1},P)&=\frac{ku_{1}\left(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}_{0}\|\right)}{\gamma_{1}}\Big(\mathbf{L}^{T}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1})\mathbf{L}\Big)^{-1}\mathbf{L}^{T}\left(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\right)\mathrm{vec}\left(\mathbf{z}_{0}\mathbf{z}_{0}^{T}\right)\\
&\qquad-\frac{1}{c_{\sigma}^{2}}\left(\frac{v_{1}(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}_{0}\|)}{\gamma_{1}}-\frac{2(\rho_{0}(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}_{0}\|)-b_{0})}{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\rho_{0}^{\prime}(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|)c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|\right]}\right)\bm{\theta}^{*},\end{split}

where
\mathbf{z}_{0}=\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}(\mathbf{y}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}^{*})
.

Note that for
\mathbf{V}
with a linear structure, one has
\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)))=\mathbf{L}\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)
.
Hence, the influence function for the covariance MM-functional follows immediately from Corollary
4
:

\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0},\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1})),P)=\mathbf{L}\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\theta}_{1},P).

(8.6)

Since
\rho_{1}^{\prime}(s)s
and
\rho_{0}(s)
are bounded,
it follows that the influence functions of
\bm{\gamma}
,
\bm{\theta}_{1}
, and
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1})

are bounded uniformly in both
\mathbf{y}_{0}
and
\mathbf{X}_{0}
,
whereas
\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0},\bm{\beta}_{1},P)
is bounded uniformly in
\mathbf{y}_{0}
, but not in
\mathbf{X}_{0}
.

The expressions for the influence function of the covariance MM-functionals in Corollary
4
and (
8.6
)
are characterized by two real-valued functions,

\alpha_{C}(s)=\frac{k\rho_{1}^{\prime}(s)}{\gamma_{1}s},\quad\beta_{C}(s)=\frac{1}{c_{\sigma}^{2}}\left(\frac{\rho_{1}^{\prime}(s)s}{\gamma_{1}}-\frac{2(\rho_{0}(s)-b_{0})}{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\rho_{0}^{\prime}(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|)c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|\right]}\right).

(8.7)

When
c_{\sigma}=1
, using that for
\mathbf{V}
with a linear structure, it holds
\mathbf{L}\bm{\theta}^{*}=\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
,
this matches with the characterization of general structured covariance functionals obtained in Lopuhaä
[
23
]
.
Such a characterization was already observed by Croux and Haesbroeck
[
6
]
for affine equivariant covariance functionals.
The function
\alpha_{C}
in (
8.7
) coincides with the one for the covariance S-functional defined with
\rho_{1}
,
see Corollary 8.4 in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
.
The function
\beta_{C}
in (
8.7
) (with
c_{\sigma}=1
) has the same structure as the one for covariance S-functionals,
but the first term is built from
\rho_{1}
, whereas the second term is built from
\rho_{0}
.
As expected, when
\rho_{0}=\rho_{1}=\rho
, the above characterization coincides with the
influence function of the covariance S-functional defined with loss function
\rho
.

Furthermore, for covariance functionals
\mathbf{C}
, it holds that
the influence function of a scale invariant mapping
H(\mathbf{C})
,
i.e.,
H(\lambda\mathbf{C})=H(\mathbf{C})
, for
\lambda>0
, only depends on the function
\alpha_{C}
,
see (8.3) in Kent and Tyler
[
14
]
for covariance CM-functionals
or see Lemma 2 in Lopuhaä
[
23
]
for linearly structured covariance functionals.
Because the characterizations of the influence functions of covariance MM- and S-functionals have
the same function
\alpha_{C}
, it follows that the influence functions of any scale invariant mapping
of covariance MM- and S-functionals are the same.
A typical example is the shape component
\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{1})
of the covariance
MM-functional, where
\bm{\Gamma}
is defined in (
3.1
).
Lemma 2 in Lopuhaä
[
23
]
, together with Corollary
4
and (
8.6
),
yields that
\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{1})
has influence function

\begin{split}\text{IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0};\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{1}),P)=\frac{ku_{1}(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}_{0}\|)}{\sigma^{2}(P)\gamma_{1}}\bigg\{\mathbf{L}\Big(\mathbf{L}^{T}(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1})\mathbf{L})\Big)^{-1}\mathbf{L}^{T}\left(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\right)\\
\mathrm{vec}\left(\mathbf{z}_{0}\mathbf{z}_{0}^{T}\right)-\frac{\|\mathbf{z}_{0}\|^{2}}{k}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\bigg\},\end{split}

(8.8)

where
\mathbf{z}_{0}=\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1/2}(\mathbf{y}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}^{*})

and
\gamma_{1}
is defined in (
8.5
).
When
c_{\sigma}=1
, then this is the same as the influence function of the shape S-functional defined with
\rho_{1}
,
see Example 6 in Lopuhaä
[
23
]
.
For the location-scatter model of Example
3
,
this confirms the claim made in Salibián-Barrera
et al

[
31
]

for the shape MM-functional.

Similarly, the influence function of a scale invariant mapping of variance component functionals
only depends on the function
\alpha_{C}
, see Lemma 2 in Lopuhaä
[
23
]
.
Since the characterizations of the variance components MM- and S-functional share the same function
\alpha_{C}
,
see Corollary
4
and Corollary 8.4 in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
,
it follows that the influence functions of any scale invariant mapping of variance component MM- and S-functionals are the same.
Examples are
\bm{\theta}/\|\bm{\theta}\|
or
\bm{\theta}/|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})|^{1/k}
, for linear covariance structures,
which represent a direction component of
\bm{\theta}
.

Remark 8.1
.

From (
3.11
) and the fact that
|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})|=1
,
it follows that
\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{1})=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})
.
This means that
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})
represents the shape component of
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1})
,
so that the expression in (
8.8
)
also coincides with the
influence function of
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})
.
Similarly, the expression for
\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s}_{0};\bm{\gamma},P)
in Theorem
6

coincides with the influence function of the direction component
\bm{\theta}_{1}/|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1})|^{1/k}
,
corresponding to the variance components MM-functional
\bm{\theta}_{1}
.
The influence function of the direction component
\bm{\theta}_{1}/\|\bm{\theta}_{1}\|

can be found in Example 7 in Lopuhaä
[
23
]
.

The results in Corollary
4
, and in (
8.6
) and (
8.8
) can be applied
to derive influence functions for the covariance functionals in the multivariate statistical models
of Examples
1
,
2
, and
3
.
Details are given in the supplemental material
[
19
]
.

9
Asymptotic normality

To establish asymptotic normality of the MM-estimators, we use the score equations
obtained in Proposition
1
.
We will use score equation (
7.2
),
with
P
replaced by the empirical measure
\mathbb{P}_{n}

corresponding to observations

\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{n}
, with
\mathbf{s}_{i}=(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
.
From Proposition
1
, we see that any local minimum
\bm{\xi}_{n}=(\bm{\beta}_{1,n},\bm{\gamma}_{n})\in\mathfrak{D}

of
R_{n}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
with
|\mathbf{V}(\gamma)|=1
, must satisfy

\int\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{n},\sigma_{n})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{n}(\mathbf{s})=\mathbf{0},

(9.1)

where
\Psi=(\Psi_{\bm{\beta}},\Psi_{\bm{\gamma}})
is defined in (
7.3
)
and
\sigma_{n}
is a solution of (
3.2
).

9.1
The general case

Since
\Psi
also depends on
\sigma
it can be expected that for general
P
,
