the limiting behavior of
\bm{\xi}_{n}
will depend on that of
\sigma_{n}
,
see Lemma
13
in the supplemental material
[
19
]
.
In turn, since
\sigma_{n}
is a solution of (
3.2
), it can be expected
that in general its limiting behavior depends on that of the initial estimators

\bm{\zeta}_{0,n}=(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\theta}_{0,n})
,
see Lemma
14
in the supplemental material
[
19
]
.

Similar to Section
8
, the situation becomes somewhat simpler if the distribution
P

has a point of symmetry.
The asymptotic expansion for
\sigma_{n}-\sigma(P)
obtained in Lemma
14

in
[
19
]
does simplify,
but details on the limiting distribution of
\bm{\xi}_{n}-\bm{\xi}(P)
can still not be provided without further information on the limiting
behavior of
\bm{\zeta}_{0,n}-\bm{\zeta}_{0}(P)
.
The situation differs for
\bm{\beta}_{1,n}-\bm{\beta}_{1}(P)
, for which the
limiting distribution is given by the following corollary.

Theorem 7
.

Suppose
\rho_{1}
satisfies (R2) and (R4), such that
u_{1}(s)
is of bounded variation.
Suppose
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V1) and
\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{s}\|^{2}<\infty

Let
\sigma_{n}
and
\sigma(P)
be solutions of (
3.2
) and (
3.8
), respectively,
and let
\bm{\xi}_{n}
and
\bm{\xi}(P)
be local minima of
R_{n}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
and

R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
, respectively.
Suppose
(\bm{\xi}_{n},\sigma_{n})\to(\bm{\xi}(P),\sigma(P))
, in probability,
and that
\bm{\beta}_{1}(P)
is a point of symmetry of
P
.
Let
\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}
be defined by (
8.3
)
with
\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}
from (
7.3
)
and suppose that
\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}
is continuously differentiable with a non-singular derivative

\mathbf{D}_{\bm{\beta}}=\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}/\partial\bm{\beta}
at
(\bm{\xi}(P),\sigma(P))
.
Then
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\beta}_{1,n}-\bm{\beta}_{1}(P))
is asymptotically normal with mean zero and variance

\mathbf{D}_{\bm{\beta}}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P),\sigma(P))\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P),\sigma(P))^{T}\right]\mathbf{D}_{\bm{\beta}}^{-1}.

The limiting distribution of
\bm{\beta}_{1,n}
given in Theorem
7

is similar to that of the regression MM-estimator
\bm{\beta}_{1}
in Lopuhaä
[
18
]

defined with loss function
\rho_{1}
and initial covariance estimator
\mathbf{V}_{0,n}
,
and they both coincide when
\mathbf{V}_{0,n}\to\sigma^{2}(P)\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P))
, in probability.

If one is allowed to interchange integration and differentiation in
\mathbf{D}_{\bm{\beta}}
,
then for the multiple linear regression model,
the limiting distribution of
\bm{\beta}_{1,n}
established in Theorem
7

coincides with that of the regression MM-estimator considered in
Yohai
[
36
]
.
For linear mixed effects models, multivariate linear regression models,
or multivariate location-scatter models, the limiting distribution of MM-estimators is only available
at distributions with an elliptically contoured density.
This situation is discussed in the next section for model (
2.1
).

9.2
Elliptical contoured densities

Consider the special case that
P
satisfies (E).
As before, when determining the limiting normal distribution of the MM-estimators,
we cannot use affine equivariance and restrict ourselves to the case
(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k})
.
Instead, we use some of the results obtained in Section
8.2

to establish the limiting normal distributions of the MM-estimators

\bm{\xi}_{n}=(\bm{\beta}_{1,n},\bm{\gamma}_{n})
,

\bm{\theta}_{1,n}
, and
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1,n})
.
Let

\sigma_{1}=\frac{k\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\rho_{1}^{\prime}(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|)^{2}(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|)^{2}\right]}{(k+2)\gamma_{1}^{2}},

(9.2)

where
c_{\sigma}=|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/(2k)}/\sigma(P)

and
\gamma_{1}
is defined in (
8.5
).
The limiting distribution of MM-estimators
\bm{\beta}_{1,n}
and
\bm{\gamma}_{n}

at distributions
P
, such that
\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{X}
has an elliptical contoured density, are now given by the following theorem.

Theorem 8
.

Suppose
P
satisfies (E) for some
(\bm{\beta}^{*},\bm{\theta}^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\bm{\Theta}

and
\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{s}\|^{2}<\infty
.
Suppose
\rho_{1}
satisfies (R1)-(R5), such that
u_{1}(s)
is of bounded variation,
and suppose
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V5) and has a linear structure (
7.5
).
Let
\sigma_{n}
and
\sigma(P)
be solutions of (
3.2
) and (
3.8
), respectively,
and suppose that
\sigma_{n}-\sigma(P)=O_{P}(1/\sqrt{n})
.
Let
\bm{\xi}_{n}=(\bm{\beta}_{1,n},\bm{\gamma}_{n})
and
\bm{\xi}(P)=(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}(P))
be local minima of
R_{n}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
and

R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
, respectively,
and suppose that
\bm{\xi}_{n}\to\bm{\xi}(P)
, in probability.
Suppose that
\bm{\beta}_{1}(P)=\bm{\beta}^{*}
and that
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P))=\mathbf{\Sigma}/|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/k}
.
Let
\alpha_{1}
and
\gamma_{1}
be defined in (
8.5
)
and suppose that
\alpha_{1}\neq 0
and
\gamma_{1}>0
.
Suppose
\mathbf{X}
has full rank with probability one
and
\mathbf{L}
, as defined in (
7.6
), has full rank.
Then
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\beta}_{1,n}-\bm{\beta}^{*})
and
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\gamma}_{n}-\bm{\gamma}(P))
are
asymptotically independent.

Furthermore,
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\beta}_{1,n}-\bm{\beta}^{*})
is asymptotically normal with mean zero and variance

\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\rho_{1}^{\prime}(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|)^{2}\right]}{c_{\sigma}^{2}k\alpha_{1}^{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\right]\right)^{-1},

where
c_{\sigma}=|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/(2k)}/\sigma(P)
,
and
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\gamma}_{n}-\bm{\gamma}(P))
is asymptotically normal with mean zero and variance

\frac{2\sigma_{1}}{|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{2/k}}\left\{\Big(\mathbf{L}^{T}\left(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\right)\mathbf{L}\Big)^{-1}-\frac{1}{k}\bm{\theta}^{*}(\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T}\right\},

where
\sigma_{1}
is defined in (
9.2
).

When
c_{\sigma}=1
,
then similar to Theorem
7
, we find that the limiting distribution of

\bm{\beta}_{1,n}
coincides with that of the regression MM-estimator considered in Lopuhaä
[
18
]
,
defined with loss function
\rho_{1}
and an initial covariance estimator
\mathbf{V}_{0,n}
that is consistent for
\mathbf{\Sigma}
.
Moreover, it also coincides with the limiting distribution of the regression S-estimator defined with loss function
\rho_{1}
,
see Corollary 9.2 in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
.
This confirms the claim made by Salibián-Barrera
et al

[
31
]

about the location MM-estimator in the model of Example
3
.

For the linear mixed effects model, the limiting distribution of
\bm{\beta}_{1,n}
obtained in Theorem
8

extends Theorem 1 in Copt and Heritier
[
4
]
, which is restricted to
\mathbf{X}_{i}=\mathbf{X}
.
For the multivariate linear regression model, the limiting distribution of
\bm{\beta}_{1,n}
in
Theorem
8
coincides with the one found for the regression MM-estimator in
Kudraszow and Maronna
[
15
]
.
This also applies to the location MM-estimator in the multivariate location-scatter model,
since this model is a special case of the multivariate linear regression model.
Furthermore, there is a connection with CM-estimators considered in
Kent and Tyler
[
14
]
,
whose limiting distribution depends on a parameter
\lambda_{0}
.
The limiting distribution of
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\beta}_{1,n}-\bm{\beta}^{*})
obtained in Theorem
8

is similar to that of the location CM-estimator, see (7.9) in Kent and Tyler
[
14
]
,
and they both coincide when
c_{\sigma}=1/\sqrt{\lambda_{0}}
.

Let

\sigma_{3}=\frac{4\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\left(\rho_{0}(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|)-b_{0}\right)^{2}\right]}{\big(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\rho_{0}^{\prime}(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|)c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|\right]\big)^{2}},

(9.3)

where
c_{\sigma}=|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/(2k)}/\sigma(P)
.
It remains to determine the limiting distribution of the variance components MM-estimator
\bm{\theta}_{1,n}
.
This is given in the next corollary.

Corollary 5
.

Suppose that the conditions hold of Theorem
8

and suppose that
\rho_{0}
satisfies (R1), (R2) and (R4).
Let
\bm{\zeta}_{0,n}=(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\theta}_{0,n})
be the pair of initial estimators
and let
\bm{\zeta}_{0}=(\bm{\beta}_{0},\bm{\theta}_{0})
be the corresponding functional.
Suppose that
(\bm{\beta}_{0}(P),\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))=(\bm{\beta}^{*},\bm{\theta}^{*})
and that
\bm{\zeta}_{0,n}-\bm{\zeta}_{0}(P)=O_{P}(1/\sqrt{n})
.
Let
\sigma_{n}
and
\sigma(P)
be solutions of (
3.2
) and (
3.8
), respectively,
and suppose that
\sigma_{n}\to\sigma(P)
, in probability.
Let
\bm{\theta}_{1,n}
and
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)
be solutions of (
3.6
) and (
3.11
), respectively,
and suppose that
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}[\rho_{0}^{\prime}(c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|)c_{\sigma}\|\mathbf{z}\|]>0
,
where
c_{\sigma}=|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/(2k)}/\sigma(P)
.
Then
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\theta}_{1,n}-\bm{\theta}_{1}(P))
is asymptotically normal with mean zero and variance

\frac{2\sigma_{1}}{c_{\sigma}^{2}}\Big(\mathbf{L}^{T}\left(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\right)\mathbf{L}\Big)^{-1}+\left(-\frac{2\sigma_{1}}{kc_{\sigma}^{2}}+\sigma_{3}\right)\bm{\theta}^{*}(\bm{\theta}^{*})^{T},

where
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{3}
are defined in (
9.2
) and (
9.3
).

For linearly structured
\mathbf{V}
, one has
\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)))=\mathbf{L}\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)
and
\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma})=\mathbf{L}\bm{\theta}^{*}
.
Hence, application of the delta-method yields that

\sqrt{n}(\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1,n}))-\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma}))
is asymptotically normal with
mean zero and variance

\frac{2\sigma_{1}}{c_{\sigma}^{2}}\mathbf{L}\Big(\mathbf{L}^{T}\left(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\right)\mathbf{L}\Big)^{-1}\mathbf{L}^{T}+\left(-\frac{2\sigma_{1}}{kc_{\sigma}^{2}}+\sigma_{3}\right)\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma})^{T}.

(9.4)

The expressions for the limiting variances of the covariance
MM-estimators in Corollary
5
and (
9.4
) are characterized by
two scalars
\sigma_{1}/c_{\sigma}^{2}
and
\sigma_{2}=-2\sigma_{1}/(kc_{\sigma}^{2})+\sigma_{3}
.
When
c_{\sigma}=1
,
this matches with the characterization of general structured covariance estimators,
see Corollary 2 in Lopuhaä
[
23
]
.
Such a characterization was already observed by Tyler
[
33
]
for
affine equivariant covariance estimators in the multivariate location-scatter model.
The constant
\sigma_{1}
(with
c_{\sigma}=1
) coincides with the one for the covariance S-estimator defined
with loss function
\rho_{1}
.
The constant
\sigma_{2}
(with
c_{\sigma}=1
) has the same structure as the one for covariance S-estimators,
but the first term
-2\sigma_{1}/k
is built from
\rho_{1}
,
whereas the second term
\sigma_{3}
is built from
\rho_{0}
.
As expected, when
\rho_{0}=\rho_{1}=\rho
, the above characterization coincides with the
one for the covariance S-estimator defined with loss function
\rho
.

Note that the limiting variance of scale invariant mappings
H(\mathbf{C}_{n})
of a covariance estimator
\mathbf{C}_{n}
,
only depends on the scalar
\sigma_{1}
, see (8.2) in Kent and Tyler
[
14
]
for
affine equivariant covariance estimators or Theorem 3 in Lopuhaä
[
23
]

for estimators of a linearly structured covariance.
Because the characterizations of the limiting variances of covariance MM- and S-estimators have
the same scalar
\sigma_{1}
, it follows that the limiting distributions of any scale invariant mapping
of covariance MM- and S-estimators are the same.
A typical example is the shape component
\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{1,n})
of the covariance MM-estimator,
where
\bm{\Gamma}
is defined in (
3.1
).
Theorem 2 and Example 4 in Lopuhaä
[
23
]
,
together with (
9.4
),
yield that
\sqrt{n}(\mathrm{vec}(\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{1,n}))-\mathrm{vec}(\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{1}(P))))

is asymptotically normal with mean zero and variance

\frac{2\sigma_{1}}{c_{\sigma}^{2}|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{2/k}}\left\{\mathbf{L}\Big(\mathbf{L}^{T}\left(\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}\right)\mathbf{L}\Big)^{-1}\mathbf{L}^{T}-\frac{1}{k}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{\Sigma})^{T}\right\}.

(9.5)

When
c_{\sigma}=1
, this coincides with the limiting distribution
of the shape S-estimator defined with
\rho_{1}
,
see Examples 3 and 4 in Lopuhaä
[
23
]
.
For the location-scatter model in Example
3
,
this confirms the claim made in Salibián-Barrera
et al

[
31
]

for the shape MM-estimator.

Similarly, the limiting distribution of a scale invariant mapping of variance component estimators
only depends on the scalar
\sigma_{1}
, see Theorem 2 in Lopuhaä
[
23
]
.
Since the characterizations of the limiting distribution of the variance components MM- and S-estimators share the same scalar
\sigma_{1}
,
see Corollary
5
and Corollary 9.2 in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
,
it follows that the limiting distributions of any scale invariant mapping of variance component MM- and S-estimators are the same.
Examples are direction components, such as
\bm{\theta}/\|\bm{\theta}\|
or
\bm{\theta}/|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})|^{1/k}
, for linear covariance structures.

Remark 9.1
.

From (
3.6
) and the fact that
|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{n})|=1
,
it follows that
\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{1,n})=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{n})
.
This means that
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{n})
represents the shape component of
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1,n})
,
so that the limiting distribution of the shape component of
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1,n})

is the same as that of
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{n})
.
Similarly, the limiting distribution of
\sqrt{n}(\bm{\gamma}_{n}-\bm{\gamma}(P))
established
in Theorem
8
,
coincides with that of the direction component
\bm{\theta}/|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})|^{1/k}
,
corresponding to the variance components MM-estimator
\bm{\theta}_{1,n}
.
The limiting distribution of the direction component
\bm{\theta}_{1,n}/\|\bm{\theta}_{1,n}\|

can be found in Example 5 in Lopuhaä
[
23
]
.

The results in Corollary
5
, and in (
9.4
)
and (
9.5
) can be applied
to derive the limiting distributions for the covariance estimators in the multivariate statistical models
of Examples
1
,
2
, and
3
.
Details are given in the supplemental material
[
19
]
.

10
Application

We apply our results to MM-estimators and MM-functionals to linear model (
2.1
).
Consider a distribution
P
that satisfies (E), where
\mathbf{V}
is has linear structure (
7.5
).
The loss functions
\rho_{0}
and
\rho_{1}
are constructed from Tukey’s biweight, as defined in (
3.7
), by taking

\rho_{j}(s)=\rho_{\text{B}}(s;c_{j})
, for
j=0,1
, such that
0<c_{0}\leq c_{1}<\infty
.
As initial estimators we use the S-estimators
(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\theta}_{0,n})
,
defined by minimizing
|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})|
, subject to

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{0}\left(d(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}))\right)=b_{0},

where
d
is defined in (
7.1
) and
b_{0}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}[\rho_{0}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)]
,
and where the minimum is taken over all
\bm{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
and
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}\subset\mathbb{R}^{l}
,
such that
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})\in\text{PDS}(k)
.
The cut-off
c_{0}
is chosen such that
b_{0}/(c_{0}^{2}/6)=0.5
, so that the initial S-estimator has (asymptotic) breakdown point 0.50.
This means that according to Theorem
8
,
the scalar
\lambda=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}[\rho_{1}^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)^{2}]/(k\alpha_{1}^{2})
,
where
\alpha_{1}
is defined in (
8.5
) with
c_{\sigma}=1
,
represents the asymptotic efficiency of the regression MM-estimator relative to
the least squares estimator (for which
\lambda=1
).
Similarly, according to (part two of) Theorem
8
and (
9.5
), the scalar
\sigma_{1}=k\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\left[\rho_{1}^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)^{2}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)^{2}\right]/((k+2)\gamma_{1}^{2})
,
where
\gamma_{1}
is defined in (
8.5
) with
c_{\sigma}=1
,
represents the asymptotic relative efficiency of both the MM-estimator of shape
as well as the MM-estimator for the direction of the variance components, relative
to the least squares estimators of shape and direction, respectively (for which
\sigma_{1}=1
).
Hence, the scalars
\lambda
and
\sigma_{1}
only depend on the function
\rho_{1}
.
By keeping
c_{0}
fixed, the breakdown point of the MM-estimators remains unaffected,
and by varying
c_{1}\geq c_{0}
we will investigate how the scalars
\lambda
and
\sigma_{1}
for the asymptotic relative efficiency
will vary.

We further investigate how at the same time the gross error sensitivity (GES) of the corresponding MM-functionals will vary.
For simplicity we only consider perturbations in
\mathbf{y}
and leave
\mathbf{X}
unchanged.
According to Theorem
6
, it can be seen that for any norm,

\|\text{IF}(\mathbf{s};\bm{\beta}_{1},P)\|
is proportional to
\alpha_{1}^{-1}\left|\rho_{1}^{\prime}(d(\mathbf{y}))\right|
,
where
\alpha_{1}
is defined in (
8.5
) with
c_{\sigma}=1
and

d(\mathbf{y})^{2}=(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}^{*})^{T}\mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}^{*})
.
Therefore, we propose the scalar

G_{1}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}\sup_{s>0}\left|\rho_{1}^{\prime}(s)\right|,

as an index for the GES of regression MM-functionals.
This coincides with the GES index for location CM-functionals in Kent and Tyler
[
14
]
.
Similarly, from (part two of) Theorem
6
and (
8.8
) it follows
that
\|\text{IF}(\mathbf{y};\bm{\gamma},P)\|
and
\|\text{IF}(\mathbf{y};\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}),P)\|
are proportional
to
\gamma_{1}^{-1}\left|\rho_{1}^{\prime}(d(\mathbf{y}))d(\mathbf{y})\right|
,
where
\gamma_{1}
is defined in (
8.5
) with
c_{\sigma}=1
.
We propose scalar

G_{2}=\frac{k}{(k+2)\gamma_{1}}\sup_{s>0}|\rho_{1}^{\prime}(s)s|,

as an index for the GES of shape and direction functionals.
In this way,
G_{2}
coincides with the GES index for CM-functionals of shape in Kent and Tyler
[
14
]
.

We investigate how the asymptotic efficiency at the multivariate normal of MM-estimators,
and the GES of the corresponding MM-functionals behave as we vary the cut-off constant
c_{1}\geq c_{0}
.

Figure 1:
Asymptotic efficiencies at the multivariate normal distribution (top row)
for the MM-estimators of regression (solid) and for shape and direction (dashed)
and the GES (bottom row) of the corresponding MM-functionals for dimensions
k=2,5,10
.

In Figure
1
, on the top row we plotted the indices
\lambda
(solid lines)
and
\sigma_{1}
(dashed lines) together as a function of
c_{1}\geq c_{0}
in dimensions
k=2,5
and 10.
In dimension
k=2
, the asymptotic efficiencies
\lambda=1.725
and
\sigma_{1}=2.656
of the MM-estimators at cut-off
c_{1}=c_{0}=2.661

are the same as that of the initial 50% breakdown S-estimators.
When increasing the cut-off
c_{1}
, one can both gain efficiency and lower the GES.
For example, the GES index for the shape and direction MM-functional attains its minimal value

G_{2}=1.344
at
c_{1}=3.724
.
For this cut-off value the GES index for the regression MM-functional is
G_{1}=1.947

and the asymptotic efficiencies are
\lambda=1.197
and
\sigma_{1}=1.383
.
Similarly, the GES index for the regression MM-functional attains its minimal value

G_{1}=1.927
at
c_{1}=4.113
.
This would yield
G_{2}=1.368
,
\lambda=1.131
and
\sigma_{1}=1.246
.

In dimension
k=5
, the asymptotic efficiencies at cut-off
c_{1}=c_{0}=4.652
are
\lambda=1.182
and
\sigma_{1}=1.285
.
The GES index for the regression MM-functional attains its minimal value
G_{1}=2.595
at
c_{1}=5.675
.
For this cut-off value the GES index
G_{2}=1.270
and the asymptotic efficiencies are

\lambda=1.073
and
\sigma_{1}=1.107
.
The index for the shape and direction MM-functional attains its minimal value

G_{2}=1.204
at
c_{1}=c_{0}=4.652
.

In dimension
k=10
, the asymptotic efficiencies at cut-off
c_{1}=c_{0}=6.776
are
\lambda=1.072
and
\sigma_{1}=1.093
.
The GES index for the regression MM-functional attains its minimal value
G_{1}=3.426
at
c_{1}=7.580
.
For this cut-off value the GES index
G_{2}=1.270
and the asymptotic efficiencies are

\lambda=1.042
and
\sigma_{1}=1.053
.
The GES index for the shape and direction MM-functional attains its minimal value

G_{2}=1.142
at
c_{1}=c_{0}=6.776
.

Figure 2:
Asymptotic efficiencies at the multivariate Student distribution with degrees of freedom
\nu=1
(top row)
and
\nu=15
(bottom row) for the MM-estimators of regression (solid) and for shape and direction (dashed)
for dimensions
k=2,5,10
.

In the top row of Figure
1
it can be seen that the asymptotic efficiencies become closer to one when the dimension is large.
This is a well known phenomenon observed when the efficiency is computed under a multivariate normal setting.
As a comparison, we have investigated whether this behavior is observed in a neighborhood of the multivariate normal.
We have computed asymptotic efficiencies relative to the maximum likelihood estimator at the
k
-variate Student distribution
with degrees of freedom
\nu=1
and
\nu=15
.
The scalars
\lambda
and
\sigma_{1}
for the ML estimator at the Student(
\nu
) distribution are given by

\lambda^{\text{ML}}=\frac{k\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[w_{1}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)^{2}\|\mathbf{z}\|^{2}]}{\Big(k\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[w_{1}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)+\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[w_{1}^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\|\mathbf{z}\|]\Big)^{2}};\quad\sigma_{1}^{\text{ML}}=\frac{k(k+2)\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[w_{1}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)^{2}\|\mathbf{z}\|^{4}]}{\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\nu}[w_{1}^{\prime}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)\|\mathbf{z}\|^{3}+k(k+2)\Big)^{2}},

where
w_{1}(s)=(\nu+k)/(k+s^{2})
.

The asymptotic efficiencies relative to the ML estimator at the
k
-variate
\text{Student}(\nu)
distribution with

\nu\in\{1,15\}
are visible in Figure
2
.
The graphs in the top row correspond to
\nu=1
and are quite different from the ones in the top row in Figure
1
.
Moreover, the behavior of the MM-regression estimator (solid lines) differs from that of the MM-estimators of shape and direction (dashed lines).
The best efficiencies for the regression MM-estimator
\lambda=1.124,1.152,1.192
, for
k=2,5,10
,
are obtained for values of
c_{1}
very close or equal to
c_{0}
,
whereas the efficiency for the MM-estimators of shape and direction can be improved for larger values of
c_{1}

and are equal to
\sigma_{1}=1.631,1.655,1.671
at
c_{1}=7.656,9.561,11.893
, for
k=2,5,10
.
As expected both MM-estimators with large values for
c_{1}
have poor efficiencies, because they tend to behave similar
to the least squares estimators.

For the Student distribution with
\nu=15
degrees of freedom, the behavior of the efficiency is more or less
in between the ones at the multivariate normal and the Student distribution with
\nu=1
degrees of freedom.
The graphs in the bottom row of Figure
2
are more similar
to the ones in the top row of Figure
1
, although in higher dimensions the efficiencies get worse.
The best efficiencies for the regression MM-estimator
\lambda=1.001,1.002,1.004
, for
k=2,5,10
,
are obtained at
c_{1}=7.246,7.925,9.070
,
and the best efficiencies for the MM-estimators of shape and direction
are equal to
\sigma_{1}=1.003,1.005,1.008
, for
k=2,5,10
,
obtained at
c_{1}=8.065,8.806,9.952
.

11
Supplemental Material

11.1
Proofs for Section
4

For any
k\times k
matrix
\mathbf{A}
, let
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{A})\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A})
denote the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{A}
.

Proof of Lemma
1

Proof.

From (R1)-(R2) we have that
\rho_{0}
is bounded and continuous at zero.
Hence, by dominated convergence, it follows that

\lim_{\sigma\to\infty}\int\rho_{0}\left(\frac{\displaystyle\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0}(P))^{T}\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0}(P))}}{\sigma}\right)\,\text{d}P(\mathbf{s})=0.

Similarly, together with (C0) and (R3), we find that

\begin{split}\lim_{\sigma\downarrow 0}\int\rho_{0}\left(\frac{\displaystyle\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0}(P))^{T}\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0}(P))}}{\sigma}\right)\,\text{d}P(\mathbf{s})\\
=(\sup\rho_{0})(1-P(E_{0}))>b_{0}.\end{split}

Since
\rho_{0}
is continuous and
0<b_{0}<\sup\rho_{0}
, we conclude that there exists a solution
\sigma(P)>0
to (
3.8
).
Because
\rho_{0}
is strictly increasing on
[0,c_{0}]
, together with (C0), it follows that
\sigma(P)
is unique.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1

Proof.

For
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{l}
,
define cylinder

\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma},c)=\left\{(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}:(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\leq c^{2}\right\}.

(11.1)

According to (V2), there exists
\bm{\gamma}_{0}\in\bm{\Theta}
, such that

\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{0})=\frac{\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))}{|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))|^{1/k}}=\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{0}(P)),

and clearly
|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{0})|=1
.
If
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma})\in\mathfrak{D}
minimizes
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
, then together with (
3.3
)
and (
3.8
),
it must satisfy

\begin{split}&P\left(\mathcal{C}\left(\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma},c_{1}\sigma(P)\right)\right)\\
&\geq 1-\frac{1}{\sup\rho_{1}}\int\rho_{1}\left(\frac{\displaystyle\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})}}{\sigma(P)}\right)\text{d}P(\mathbf{s})\\
&\geq 1-\frac{1}{\sup\rho_{1}}\int\rho_{1}\left(\frac{\displaystyle\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0}(P))^{T}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{0})^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0}(P))}}{\sigma(P)}\right)\text{d}P(\mathbf{s})\\
&\geq 1-\frac{1}{\sup\rho_{0}}\int\rho_{0}\left(\frac{\displaystyle\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0}(P))^{T}\bm{\Gamma}_{0}(P)^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0}(P))}}{\sigma(P)}\right)\text{d}P(\mathbf{s})\\
&=1-\frac{b_{0}}{\sup\rho_{0}}=1-r_{0}\geq\epsilon.\end{split}

(11.2)

Since
P
satisfies
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
for
1-r_{0}\geq\epsilon
,
from Lemma 4.1(i) in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
,
it follows that there exist
a_{1}>0
, only depending on
c_{1}
and
(\mathrm{C2}_{\epsilon})
,
such that
\lambda_{k}(\sigma^{2}(P)\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))\geq a_{1}
, so that
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))\geq a_{1}/\sigma^{2}(P)>0
.
Since
|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})|=1
, it immediately follows that

\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))\leq a_{2}:=\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}(P)}{a_{1}}\right)^{k-1}<\infty.

Let
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
be a compact set, such that
P(K)\geq r_{0}+\epsilon
, which exists according to condition
(\mathrm{C1}_{\epsilon})
.
From Lemma 4.1(iii) in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
,
it follows that
\|\bm{\beta}\|\leq M<\infty
, for some
M>0
that only depends on
c_{1}
,
a_{2}
,
\sigma(P)
,
K
and
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
.
We conclude that
\bm{\beta}
is in a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{q}
and
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})

is in a compact set
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
.
By identifiability, the mapping
\bm{\gamma}\mapsto\mathbf{V}(\gamma)
is one-to-one,
so we can restrict
\bm{\gamma}
to the pre-image
\mathbf{V}^{-1}(B)
.
Then with conditions (V1) and (V3), it follows that also
\mathbf{V}^{-1}(B)
is a compact set in
\bm{\Theta}
.
We conclude that for minimizing
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))

we can restrict ourselves to a compact set
B^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{D}
.

Because
\rho_{1}
is continuous, together with condition (V1) and dominated convergence,
it follows that
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
is a continuous function of

(\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma})
, so that it must attain a minimum on
B^{\prime}
.
Hence, there exists a pair
(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}(P))\in\mathfrak{D}
that minimizes
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
.
Finally, condition (V2) immediately yields that there exists a
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)
that solves (
3.11
)
and by identifiability it follows that
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)
is unique.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{P}_{n}
be the empirical measure corresponding to the collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.
Then
\mathbb{P}_{n}
satisfies
(\text{C1}_{\epsilon})
for any
0<\epsilon\leq 1-r_{0}
and
satisfies
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
, for
\epsilon=(\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})+1)/n
.
Clearly,
0<(\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})+1)/n\leq 1-r_{0}
, where
r_{0}=b_{0}/\sup\rho_{0}
.
Furthermore, since
(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\theta}_{0,n})
satisfies (
3.12
),
it follows that
\sigma_{n}=\sigma(\mathbb{P}_{n})
is a solution of (
3.8
), with
P=\mathbb{P}_{n}
.
Hence, according to Theorem
1
there exists
a pair
(\bm{\beta}_{1}(\mathbb{P}_{n}),\bm{\gamma}(\mathbb{P}_{n}))
that minimizes
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
, with
P=\mathbb{P}_{n}
,
and a vector
\bm{\theta}_{1}(\mathbb{P}_{n})
that is the unique solution of (
3.11
), with
P=\mathbb{P}_{n}
.
But this is equivalent with saying that there exists a pair
(\bm{\beta}_{1,n},\bm{\gamma}_{n})\in\mathfrak{D}
that minimizes
R_{n}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))

and a vector
\bm{\theta}_{1,n}
that is the unique solution of (
3.6
).
∎

Proof of Corollary
2

Proof.

Because
P
satisfies condition (C3), according to Ranga Rao
[
27
, Theorem 4.2]
we have

\sup_{C\in\mathfrak{C}}\left|P_{t}(C)-P(C)\right|\to 0,\quad\text{as }t\to\infty.

(11.3)

Consider the set
E_{t,0}=\left\{(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}:\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0}(P_{t})\|=0\right\}
.
Then
E_{t,0}\in\mathfrak{C}
,
so that
P_{t}(E_{t,0})-P(E_{t,0})\to 0
, as
t\to\infty
.
Since
\bm{\beta}_{0}(P_{t})\to\bm{\beta}_{0}(P)
, as
t\to\infty
, it follows that
P(E_{t,0})\to P(E_{0})
,
which implies that

P_{t}(E_{t,0})=P_{t}(E_{t,0})-P(E_{t,0})+P(E_{t,0})\to P(E_{0})<1-\frac{b_{0}}{\sup\rho_{0}}.

Therefore,
P_{t}
satisfies (C0), for
t
sufficiently large.
According to Lemma
1
, a solution
\sigma(P_{t})

of (
3.8
) with
P=P_{t}
exists and is unique.
This proves part(i).

The argument that minimizing

R_{P_{t}}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
over
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma})\in\mathfrak{D}

has at least one solution, is similar to the proof of Corollary 4.4 in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
.
First note there exists
0<\eta<\epsilon^{\prime}-\epsilon
.
Because strips
H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,\delta)\in\mathfrak{C}
,
property (
11.3
) implies that every strip with
P_{t}(H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,\delta))\geq\epsilon+\eta

must also satisfy
P(H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,\delta))\geq\epsilon
,
for
t
sufficiently large.
Together with the fact that
P
satisfies
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
,
this means that, for
t
sufficiently large,

\inf\left\{\delta:P_{t}(H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,\delta))\geq\epsilon+\eta\right\}\geq\inf\left\{\delta:P(H(\bm{\alpha},\ell,\delta))\geq\epsilon\right\}>0.

It follows that, for
t
sufficiently large,
P_{t}
satisfies condition
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon+\eta})
.
Next, consider the compact set
K
from (
\text{C1}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
).
Without loss of generality we may assume that it belongs to
\mathfrak{C}
.
Therefore,
as
P(K)\geq r_{0}+\epsilon^{\prime}
, for
t
sufficiently large
P_{t}(K)\geq r_{0}+\epsilon+\eta
.
It follows that, for
t
sufficiently large,
P_{t}
satisfies condition
(\text{C1}_{\epsilon+\eta})
.
Since
\epsilon+\eta<1-r_{0}
, according to Theorem
1
,
for
t
sufficiently large,
there exists a pair
(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P_{t}),\bm{\gamma}(P_{t}))\in\mathfrak{D}
that minimizes
R_{P_{t}}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))

and a vector
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P_{t})
that is the unique solution of (
3.11
) with
P=P_{t}
.
This proves part(ii).
∎

11.2
Proofs for Section
5

Proof of Theorem
2

Proof.

Let
\bm{\beta}_{0,t}=\bm{\beta}_{0}(P_{t})
and
\bm{\beta}_{0,P}=\bm{\beta}_{0}(P)
.
Let
\bm{\Gamma}
be the functional defined in (
3.1
),
and define
\bm{\Gamma}_{0,t}=\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{0}(P_{t}))
and
\bm{\Gamma}_{0,P}=\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))
.
Since
\rho_{0}
satisfies (R2)-(R3) and
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V1),
we can apply Lemma 3.2 from Lopuhaä
[
20
]
.
As
(\bm{\beta}_{0,t},\bm{\Gamma}_{0,t})\to(\bm{\beta}_{0,P},\bm{\Gamma}_{0,P})
,
it follows that for
s
fixed,

\int\rho_{0}\left(\frac{d(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0,t},\bm{\Gamma}_{0,t})}{s}\right)\,\text{d}P_{t}(\mathbf{s})\to\int\rho_{0}\left(\frac{d(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0,P},\bm{\Gamma}_{0,P})}{s}\right)\,\text{d}P(\mathbf{s}),

(11.4)

where
d
is defined in (
7.1
).
Let
\sigma(P)
be the unique solution of (
3.8
).
Let
\delta>0
and suppose that
\liminf_{t\to\infty}\sigma(P_{t})>\sigma(P)+\delta
.
Since
\rho_{0}
is strictly increasing on
[0,c_{0}]
,
together with (
11.4
),
it follows that

\begin{split}\int\rho_{0}\left(\frac{d(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0,t},\bm{\Gamma}_{0,t})}{\sigma(P_{t})}\right)\,\text{d}P_{t}(\mathbf{s})&\leq\int\rho_{0}\left(\frac{d(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0,t},\bm{\Gamma}_{0,t})}{\sigma(P)+\delta}\right)\,\text{d}P_{t}(\mathbf{s})\\
&\to\int\rho_{0}\left(\frac{d(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0,P},\bm{\Gamma}_{0,P})}{\sigma(P)+\delta}\right)\,\text{d}P(\mathbf{s})\\
&<\int\rho_{0}\left(\frac{d(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0,P},\bm{\Gamma}_{0,P})}{\sigma(P)}\right)\,\text{d}P(\mathbf{s})=b_{0},\end{split}

which is in contradiction with the definition of
\sigma(P_{t})
.
The argument is similar for
\sigma(P_{t})<\sigma(P)-\delta
.
We conclude that
|\sigma(P_{t})-\sigma(P)|<\delta
, for
t
sufficiently large.
Since
\delta>0
is arbitrary, this means that
\sigma(P_{t})\to\sigma(P)
.
This proves part (i).

To prove part (ii),
first note that there exists
0<\eta<\epsilon^{\prime}-\epsilon
.
Because
(\bm{\beta}_{1,t},\bm{\gamma}_{t})=(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P_{t}),\bm{\gamma}(P_{t}))

is a local minimum of
R_{P_{t}}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))

that satisfies (
3.10
), we have

R_{P_{t}}(\bm{\beta}_{1,t},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{t}))\leq R_{P_{t}}(\bm{\beta}_{0,t},\bm{\Gamma}_{0,t}).

(11.5)

Then, together with (
3.3
), similar to (
11.2
)
we find that

P_{t}(\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{1,t},\bm{\gamma}_{t},c_{1}\sigma(P_{t})))\geq 1-r_{0}
.
Therefore, since
P
satisfies (C3) and
\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{1,t},\bm{\gamma}_{t},c_{1}\sigma(P_{t}))\in\mathfrak{C}
,
and
1-r_{0}>1-r_{0}-\eta
,
it follows from (
11.3
) that

\begin{split}P(\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{1,t},\bm{\gamma}_{t},c_{1}\sigma(P_{t}))&\geq P_{t}(\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{1,t},\bm{\gamma}_{t},c_{1}\sigma(P_{t})))-\sup_{C\in\mathfrak{C}}\left|P_{t}(C)-P(C)\right|\\
&\geq 1-r_{0}-\eta,\end{split}

(11.6)

for
t
sufficiently large.
Since
1-r_{0}-\eta>\epsilon
,
according to Lemma 4.1(i) in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
,
there exists
a_{1}>0
only depending only depending on
c_{1}
and
(\mathrm{C2}_{\epsilon})
, such that

\lambda_{k}(\sigma^{2}(P_{t})\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{t}))\geq a_{1}
.
Hence,

\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{t}))\geq\frac{a_{1}}{\sigma^{2}(P_{t})},

for
t
sufficiently large.
Since
|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{t})|=1
, it immediately follows that

\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{t}))\leq\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}(P_{t})}{a_{1}}\right)^{k-1}.

Because
\sigma(P_{t})\to\sigma(P)
according to part (i), there exists
0<L_{1}<L_{2}<\infty
, such that

L_{1}\leq\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{t}))\leq\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{t}))\leq L_{2},

(11.7)

for
t
sufficiently large.
Let
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathcal{X}
be a compact set, such that
P(K)\geq r_{0}+\epsilon^{\prime}\geq r_{0}+\epsilon+\eta
,
which exists according to condition
(\mathrm{C1}_{\epsilon^{\prime}})
.
Then according to (
11.6
), it follows from
Lemma 4.1(iii) in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]

with
a=1-r_{0}-\eta
, that
\|\bm{\beta}_{1,t}\|\leq M<\infty
,
for some
M>0
that only depends on
c_{1}
,
a_{2}
,
\sigma(P)
,
K
and
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
.
We conclude that for
t
sufficiently large the sequence
\{\bm{\beta}_{1,t}\}

lies in a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{q}
and
the sequence
\{\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{t})\}
lies in a compact set
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
.
Then similar to the second part of the proof of Theorem
1
,
conditions (V1) and (V3) yield there exists a compact set
B^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{q+l}
,
such that for
t
sufficiently large
the sequence
\{(\bm{\beta}_{1,t},\bm{\gamma}_{t})\}\subset B^{\prime}
.

Since
\rho_{1}
satisfies (R2)-(R3),
together with of part (i), similar to (
11.4
),
for fixed
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\bm{\Theta}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{l}
,

R_{P_{t}}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))\to R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})),

(11.8)

where
R_{P}
is defined in (
3.9
).
For the sake of brevity, let us write
R_{t}=R_{P_{t}}
.
Since the sequence
\{(\bm{\beta}_{1,t},\bm{\gamma}_{t})\}
lies in a compact set,
it has a convergent subsequence

(\bm{\beta}_{1,t_{j}},\bm{\gamma}_{t_{j}})\to(\bm{\beta}_{1,L},\bm{\gamma}_{L})
.
Since
\rho_{1}
satisfies (R2)-(R3) and
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V1),
similar to (
11.4
), it follows that

\lim_{j\to\infty}R_{t_{j}}(\bm{\beta}_{1,t_{j}},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{t_{j}}))=R_{P}(\bm{\beta}_{1,L},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{L})).

Now, suppose that
(\bm{\beta}_{1,L},\bm{\gamma}_{L})\neq(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}(P))
.
Then, since
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
is uniquely minimized at
(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}(P))
,
this would mean that there exists
\epsilon>0
, such that together with (
11.8
),

\begin{split}R_{t_{j}}(\bm{\beta}_{1,t_{j}},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{t_{j}}))&>R_{P}(\bm{\beta}_{1,L},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{L}))-\epsilon\geq R_{P}(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P)))+2\epsilon\\
&\geq R_{t_{j}}(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P)))+\epsilon>R_{t_{j}}(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P))),\end{split}

for
t_{j}
sufficiently large.
This would mean that
(\bm{\beta}_{1,t_{j}},\bm{\gamma}_{t_{j}})
is not the minimizer of
R_{t_{j}}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
.
We conclude that
(\bm{\beta}_{1,L},\bm{\gamma}_{L})=(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}(P))
, which proves part (ii).

Finally, from part (i) and (V1), we have that

\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1}(P_{t}))=\sigma^{2}(P_{t})\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P_{t}))\to\sigma^{2}(P)\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P))=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)).

Because
\mathbf{V}
is continuous and one-to-one, part (iii) follows.
∎

Proof of Corollary
3

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{P}_{n}
be the empirical measure corresponding to the collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.
According to the Portmanteau Theorem (e.g., see Theorem 2.1 in
[
2
]
),

\mathbb{P}_{n}
converges weakly to
P
, with probability one.
Because
(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\theta}_{0,n})
satisfies (
3.12
) ,
it follows that
(\bm{\beta}_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{n}),\bm{\theta}_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{n}))=(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\theta}_{0,n})\to(\bm{\beta}_{0}(P),\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))

and that
\sigma_{n}=\sigma(\mathbb{P}_{n})
the unique solution of (
3.8
), with
P=\mathbb{P}_{n}
.
Hence, part (i) follows immediately from Theorem
2
(i).
Furthermore,
(\bm{\beta}_{1,n},\bm{\gamma}_{n})=(\bm{\beta}_{1}(\mathbb{P}_{n}),\bm{\gamma}(\mathbb{P}_{n}))

is a local minimum of
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
, with
P=\mathbb{P}_{n}
,
that satisfies (
3.10
), for
P=\mathbb{P}_{n}
.
Therefore, part (ii) follows immediately from Theorem
2
(ii).
Finally, since
\bm{\theta}_{1,n}=\bm{\theta}_{1}(\mathbb{P}_{n})
,
part (iii) follows immediately from Theorem
2
(iii).
∎

Proof of Theorem
3

Proof.

First consider the multivariate location-scatter M-functional with auxiliary scale at the distribution
F
of
\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{X}
, for some
\mathbf{X}
fixed.
So
F
has density
f_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}
from (
5.1
).
Tatsuoka and Tyler
[
32
]
define location-scale M-functionals with auxiliary scale by means of a function

\widetilde{\rho}:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
.
It relates to our
\rho_{1}
-function as
\rho_{1}(d)=\widetilde{\rho}(d^{2})
.
The M-functionals with auxiliary scale
\sigma(F)
are defined
to be
\bm{\alpha}(F)
and
\mathbf{A}(F)=\sigma^{2}(F)\mathbf{G}(F)
, where
(\bm{\alpha}(F),\mathbf{G}(F))
minimizes

\int\rho_{1}\left(\frac{\displaystyle\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}-\bm{\alpha})^{T}\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\bm{\alpha})}}{\sigma(F)}\right)f_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y},

(11.9)

over all
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
and
\mathbf{G}\in\text{PDS}(k)
with
|\mathbf{G}|=1
.
Our conditions (R1)-(R2) on
\rho_{1}

imply Condition 2.1 on
\widetilde{\rho}
imposed in Tatsuoka and Tyler
[
32
]
.
It then follows from Theorem 4.2 in Tatsuoka and Tyler
[
32
]
, together with the affine equivariance of the M-functional,
that
for any minimizer
(\bm{\alpha}(F),\mathbf{G}(F))
,
the multivariate M-functional
(\bm{\alpha}(F),\mathbf{A}(F))=(\bm{\alpha}(F),\sigma^{2}(F)\mathbf{G}(F))
has a unique
solution

(\bm{\alpha}(F),\mathbf{A}(F))=(\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma})=(\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}^{*},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}^{*})).

Since this solution is unique, any candidate minimizer of (
11.9
) must
be of the form

(\bm{\alpha},\mathbf{G})=\left(\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta},\frac{\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta})}{\sigma^{2}(F)}\right)=\left(\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})\right),

for some
\bm{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
and
\bm{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
,
where we use that
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V2).
Furthermore,

|\mathbf{\Sigma}|=|\mathbf{A}(F)|=\sigma^{2k}(F)|\mathbf{G}(F)|=\sigma^{2k}(F),

so that
\sigma(F)=|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/(2k)}
.
Then, for
\mathbf{X}
fixed, minimizing (
11.9
) over all
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
and
\mathbf{G}\in\text{PDS}(k)
with
|\mathbf{G}|=1
is equivalent to
minimizing

\int\rho_{1}\left(\frac{d(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))}{|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/(2k)}}\right)f_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y},

(11.10)

over
\bm{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
and
\bm{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
, such that
|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})|=1
,
where
d
is defined in (
7.1
).
As a consequence, for
\mathbf{X}
fixed, this minimization problem has a unique solution
(\widetilde{\bm{\beta}},\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}})
,
which satisfies
\mathbf{X}\widetilde{\bm{\beta}}=\bm{\mu}=\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}^{*}
and

\mathbf{V}(\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}})=\frac{\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}^{*})}{\sigma^{2}(F)}=\frac{\mathbf{\Sigma}}{|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/k}}.

(11.11)

Since
\mathbf{X}
has full rank, with probability one, it follows that

\widetilde{\bm{\beta}}=\bm{\beta}^{*}
.

We can transfer this result to the minimization of
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
as follows.
Because
P
is absolutely continuous, it satisfies condition (C0).
Hence, according to Lemma
1
the solution
\sigma(P)
of (
3.8
) is unique.
Because
\bm{\beta}_{0}(P)=\bm{\beta}^{*}
and
\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))=\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}^{*})=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}^{*})/|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}^{*})|^{1/k}=\mathbf{\Sigma}/|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/k}
,
in (
3.8
) we have that

\int\rho_{0}\left(\frac{d(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{0}(P),\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{0}(P)))}{\sigma}\right)\,\text{d}P(\mathbf{s})=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\rho_{0}\left(\frac{d(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}^{*},\mathbf{\Sigma})}{\sigma/|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/(2k)}}\right)\bigg|\mathbf{X}\right]\right].

The inner expectation is the conditional expectation of
\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{X}
,
which has the same distribution as
\mathbf{\Sigma}^{1/2}\mathbf{z}+\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}^{*}
,
where
\mathbf{z}
has density
f_{\textbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}
.
This means that we must solve
\sigma(P)
from

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\rho_{0}\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{z}\|}{\sigma/|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/(2k)}}\right)=b_{0}.

(11.12)

Furthermore, since
P
is absolutely continuous, it satisfies
satisfies
(\text{C1}_{\epsilon})
and
(\text{C2}_{\epsilon})
, for some
0<\epsilon\leq 1-r_{0}
,
where
r_{0}=b_{0}/\sup\rho_{0}
.
Hence, since
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V1)-(V3), according to Theorem
1
there exists
(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}(P))\in\mathfrak{D}

that minimizes
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
and
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)\in\bm{\Theta}
that is the unique solution of (
3.11
).
But then, there must be an
\mathbf{X}
such that

\begin{split}\int\rho_{1}\left(\frac{d(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P)))}{\sigma(P)}\right)f_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\leq\int\rho_{1}\left(\frac{d(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X}\widetilde{\bm{\beta}},\mathbf{V}(\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}))}{\sigma(P)}\right)f_{\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma}}(\mathbf{y})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}.\end{split}

But since for any
\mathbf{X}
fixed,
(\widetilde{\bm{\beta}},\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}})
is the unique minimizer of (
11.10
),
with probability one, we must have
\bm{\beta}_{1}(P)=\widetilde{\bm{\beta}}=\bm{\beta}^{*}
and
\bm{\gamma}(P)=\widetilde{\bm{\gamma}}
,
with probability one.
Together with (
11.11
) this proves part(i).

To prove part (ii), note that
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)
satisfies

\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1}(P))=\sigma^{2}(P)\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P))=\frac{\sigma^{2}(P)}{|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/k}}\mathbf{\Sigma}=\frac{\sigma^{2}(P)}{|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/k}}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}^{*})=\mathbf{V}\left(\frac{\bm{\theta}^{*}\sigma^{2}(P)}{|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/k}}\right),

where we use that
\mathbf{V}
has a linear structure.
By identifiability
this means that
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)=\bm{\theta}^{*}\sigma^{2}(P)/|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/k}
.

Finally, for part (iii) suppose that
b_{0}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{k}}\rho_{0}(\|\mathbf{z}\|)
.
It follows immediately from solving (
11.12
) that
\sigma(P)=|\mathbf{\Sigma}|^{1/(2k)}
.
This finishes the proof.
∎

11.3
Proofs for Section
6

Proof of Theorem
4

Proof.

Suppose we replace
m
points, where
m
is such that

\begin{split}m&\leq\min\Big(\lceil nr_{0}\rceil,\lceil n-nr_{0}\rceil-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n}),n\epsilon_{n}^{*}(\bm{\beta}_{0,n},\bm{\theta}_{0,n},\mathcal{S}_{n})\Big)-1\end{split}

Let
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
be the corrupted collection of points.
Write
(\bm{\beta}_{0,m},\bm{\theta}_{0,m})=(\bm{\beta}_{0,n}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}),\bm{\theta}_{0,n}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}))
,

\mathbf{V}_{0,m}=\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{0,n}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}))
,

\bm{\Gamma}_{0,m}=\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta}_{0,n}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}))
.
Let
\mathbb{P}_{m}^{\prime}
be the empirical measure corresponding to the
corrupted collection
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
of
n
points.
We must show that there exists a pair

(\bm{\beta}_{1,m},\bm{\gamma}_{m})=(\bm{\beta}_{1,n}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}),\bm{\gamma}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}))\in\mathfrak{D}

that satisfies (
3.5
), for the corrupted collection
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
,
and
\bm{\theta}_{1,m}=\bm{\theta}_{1,n}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime})\in\bm{\Theta}

that satisfies
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1,m})=\sigma_{m}^{2}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{m})
,
and that all pairs
(\bm{\beta}_{1,m},\bm{\gamma}_{m})
satisfying (
3.5
) and
\bm{\theta}_{1,m}
do not break down.

We first show that a solution
\sigma_{m}=\sigma_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime})
of
equation (
3.2
) exists, for the collection
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
.
Note that the maximum number of points of
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
that lie in the same hyperplane
is
m+\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})
.
Because
m\leq\lceil n-nr_{0}\rceil-\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})-1
, it follows that

m+\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})\leq\lceil n-nr_{0}\rceil-1<n(1-r_{0}).

This means that
\mathbb{P}_{m}^{\prime}
satisfies condition (C0).
Since
\rho_{0}
satisfies (R1)-(R3), according to Lemma
1
,
there exists a unique solution
\sigma_{m}>0
of (
3.2
).
Furthermore, from (R1)-(R3) we have that
\rho_{0}(s)
, for
s\in[0,c_{0}]
,
varies continuously between zero and
\sup\rho_{0}
.
Since
m\leq\lceil nr_{0}\rceil-1<nr_{0}
, there exists
\eta>0
, such that

\frac{m}{n}\sup\rho_{0}+\eta<b_{0}.

Because
\bm{\beta}_{0,m}
and
\bm{\theta}_{0,m}
do not break down, there exist
M>0
and
0<L_{1}\leq L_{2}<\infty
,
not depending on
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
,
such that
\|\bm{\beta}_{0,m}\|\leq M
and
L_{1}\leq\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{V}_{0,m})\leq\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}_{0,m})\leq L_{2}
.
This means that for all
\mathbf{s}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
:

\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta}_{0,m})^{T}\bm{\Gamma}_{0,m}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta}_{0,m})}\leq\frac{\|\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta}_{0,m}\|}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}(\bm{\Gamma}_{0,m})}}\leq\frac{\|\mathbf{y}_{i}\|+\|\mathbf{X}_{i}\|\|\bm{\beta}_{0,m}\|}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}(\bm{\Gamma}_{0,m})}},

where

\lambda_{k}(\bm{\Gamma}_{0,m})=\frac{\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{V}_{0,m})}{|\mathbf{V}_{0,m}|^{1/k}}\geq\frac{L_{1}}{(L_{2}^{k})^{1/k}}=\frac{L_{1}}{L_{2}}.

Hence, for all
\mathbf{s}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
, we obtain

d_{i,0}=\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta}_{0,m})^{T}\bm{\Gamma}_{0,m}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta}_{0,m})}\leq\left(\max_{\mathbf{s}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\|\mathbf{y}_{i}\|+M\max_{\mathbf{s}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\|\mathbf{X}_{i}\|\right)\sqrt{L_{2}/L_{1}}.

This means, there exists
0<K<\infty
, only depending on
\mathcal{S}_{n}
,
such that
\max_{\mathbf{s}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}d_{i,0}\leq K
.
Since
\rho_{0}
is continuous and
0<\eta<b_{0}
, we can define
\delta>0
, such that
\rho_{0}(\delta)=\eta
.
Let
s_{0}=K/\delta
.
Then,

\begin{split}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\rho_{0}\left(\frac{d_{i,0}}{s_{0}}\right)&=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{S}_{n}}\rho_{0}\left(\frac{d_{i,0}}{s_{0}}\right)+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{n}}\rho_{0}\left(\frac{d_{i,0}}{s_{0}}\right)\\
&\leq\frac{n-m}{n}\rho_{0}\left(\frac{K}{s_{0}}\right)+\frac{m}{n}\sup\rho_{0}\\
&\leq\rho_{0}(\delta)+\frac{m}{n}\sup\rho_{0}\\
&=\eta+\frac{m}{n}\sup\rho_{0}<b_{0}.\end{split}

As
\sigma_{m}
is the solution of (
3.2
),
we must have that
\sigma_{m}\leq s_{0}
.

Next, we show that there exists a pair

(\bm{\beta}_{1,m},\bm{\gamma}_{m})=(\bm{\beta}_{1,n}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}),\bm{\gamma}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}))\in\mathfrak{D}

that minimizes

R_{m}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\mathbf{s}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\rho_{1}\left(\frac{\displaystyle\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})^{-1}(\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{X}_{i}\bm{\beta})}}{\sigma_{m}}\right).

Any minimizer
(\bm{\beta}_{1,m},\bm{\gamma}_{m})
of
R_{m}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
must satisfy (
3.5
).
Together with (
3.3
)
similar to (
11.2
), this implies

\mathbb{P}_{m}^{\prime}\left(\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{1,m},\bm{\gamma}_{m},c_{1}\sigma_{m})\right)\geq 1-r_{0}.

It then follows that the cylinder
\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{1,m},\bm{\gamma}_{m},c_{1}\sigma_{m})

contains at least
\lceil n-nr_{0}\rceil
number of points from the corrupted collection
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, any corrupted collection
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{m}
contains

\lceil n-nr_{0}\rceil-m\geq\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})+1

(11.13)

points of the original collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.
This means that the cylinder
\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{1,m},\bm{\gamma}_{m},c_{1}\sigma_{m})

must contain a non-empty simplex only depending on the original collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.
This implies that

\lambda_{k}(\sigma_{m}^{2}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{m}))\geq a_{1}>0,

(11.14)

where
a_{1}
only depends on the original collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.
Hence,

\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{m}))\geq a_{1}/s_{0}^{2}>0
.
Since
|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{m})|=1
, it immediately follows that

\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{m}))\leq(s_{0}^{2}/a_{1})^{k-1}<\infty
.
Furthermore, recall that

\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{1,m},\bm{\gamma}_{m},c_{1}\sigma_{m})
contains a subset
J_{0}
of

\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})+1
points from the original collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
,
according to (
11.13
).
By definition,
\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})+1
original points cannot be on the same hyperplane, so that

\alpha_{n}=\inf_{J\subset\mathcal{S}_{n}}\inf_{\|\bm{\alpha}\|=1}\max_{\mathbf{s}\in J}\|\mathbf{X}\bm{\alpha}\|>0.

where the first infimum runs over all subsets
J\subset\mathcal{S}_{n}
of
\kappa(\mathcal{S}_{n})+1
points.
By definition of
\alpha_{n}
,
there exists an original point
\mathbf{s}_{0}\in J_{0}\subset\mathcal{S}_{n}\cap\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{1,m},\bm{\gamma}_{m},c_{1}\sigma_{m})
, such that

\|\bm{\beta}_{1,m}\|=\|\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}_{1,m}\|\times\frac{\|\bm{\beta}_{1,m}\|}{\|\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}_{1,m}\|}\leq\frac{1}{\alpha_{n}}\|\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}_{1,m}\|.

Because
\mathbf{s}_{0}\in\mathcal{C}(\bm{\beta}_{1,m},\bm{\gamma}_{m},c_{1}\sigma_{m})
,
it follows that

\begin{split}\|\mathbf{y}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}_{1,m}\|^{2}&\leq(\mathbf{y}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}_{1,m})^{T}(\sigma_{m}^{2}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{m}))^{-1}(\mathbf{y}_{0}-\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}_{1,m})\\
&\leq c_{1}^{2}\lambda_{1}(\sigma_{m}^{2}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{m}))\leq a_{2},\end{split}

where
a_{2}=c_{1}^{2}s_{0}^{2}(s_{0}^{2}/a_{1})^{k-1}
only depends on the original collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.
Because
\mathbf{s}_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
, we have that

\|\mathbf{X}_{0}\bm{\beta}_{1,m}\|\leq\sqrt{a_{2}}+\max_{(\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{X}_{i})\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\|\mathbf{y}_{i}\|<\infty.

We conclude that there exists a compact set of
\mathbb{R}^{q}
that contains
\bm{\beta}_{1,m}

and a compact set
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
that contains
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{m})
.
This means that
\bm{\gamma}_{m}
is in the pre-image
\mathbf{V}^{-1}(B)
,
and with conditions (V1) and (V3), it follows that
\mathbf{V}^{-1}(B)
is a compact set in
\bm{\Theta}
.
We conclude that for minimizing
R_{m}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))

we can restrict ourselves to a compact set
B^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{D}
, only depending on the original collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.
Because
\rho_{1}
and
\mathbf{V}
are continuous, it follows that

R_{m}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
is a continuous function of
(\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma})
,
so it attains a minimum on
B^{\prime}
.
This also means that there exists a pair
(\bm{\beta}_{1,m},\bm{\gamma}_{m})\in\mathfrak{D}

that satisfies (
3.5
), for the corrupted collection
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
.
Now, consider any pair
(\bm{\beta}_{1,m},\bm{\gamma}_{m})
that satisfies (
3.5
).
Then the reasoning above yields that there is a compact set
B^{\prime}
,
only depending on the original collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
, that contains
(\bm{\beta}_{1,m},\bm{\gamma}_{m})
.
Hence,
\bm{\beta}_{1,m}
and
\bm{\gamma}_{m}
do not break down.

Finally, because
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V2), there exists

\bm{\theta}_{1,m}=\bm{\theta}_{1,n}(\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime})\in\bm{\Theta}
that satisfies
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1,m})=\sigma_{m}^{2}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{m})
.
From (
11.14
), we have that

\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1,m}))=\lambda_{k}(\sigma_{m}^{2}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{m}))\geq a_{1}>0
,
where
a_{1}
only depends on the original collection
\mathcal{S}_{n}
.
Furthermore,

\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1,m}))=\sigma_{m}^{2}\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{m}))\leq s_{0}^{2}\left(\frac{s_{0}^{2}}{a_{1}}\right)^{k-1}<\infty.

This means that
\bm{\theta}_{1,m}
does not break down.
∎

11.4
Proofs for Section
7

Proof of Proposition
1

Proof.

In STAGE 3 for the MM-functional, we are considering local minima
of
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
that satisfy
|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})|=1
,
or equivalently
\log|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})|=0
.
The Lagrangian corresponding to this constrained minimization problem
is given by

L_{P}(\bm{\xi},\lambda)=R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))-\lambda\log|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})|.

Suppose
\bm{\xi}(P)=(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}(P))
is a local minimum of
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
.
Then this is also a zero of the partial derivatives

\partial L_{P}/\partial\bm{\beta}
,

\partial L_{P}/\partial\bm{\gamma}
,
and
\partial L_{P}/\partial\lambda
.
Let us write
\sigma_{P}=\sigma(P)
and consider

R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))=\int\rho_{1}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma_{P}}\right)\,\text{d}P(\mathbf{s})

where
d=d(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
, as defined in (
7.1
).
We find that

\begin{split}\frac{\partial\rho_{1}\left(d/\sigma_{P}\right)}{\partial\bm{\beta}}&=\frac{1}{2\sigma_{P}}u_{1}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma_{P}}\right)\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\\
\frac{\partial\rho_{1}\left(d/\sigma_{P}\right)}{\partial\gamma_{j}}&=\frac{1}{2\sigma_{P}}u_{1}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma_{P}}\right)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}),\end{split}

for
j=1,\ldots,l
.
Similar to the proof of Lemma 11.2 in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
,
using that
\rho_{1}
and
\mathbf{V}
satisfy (R2), (R4) and (V4), respectively,
we find that at
\bm{\xi}(P)
it holds that

\|\partial\rho_{1}\left(d_{P}/\sigma_{P}\right)/\partial\bm{\beta}\|\leq C_{1}\|\mathbf{X}\|

and

\|\partial\rho_{1}\left(d_{P}/\sigma_{P}\right)/\partial\gamma_{j}\|\leq C_{2}
,
for universal constants
0<C_{1},C_{2}<\infty
only depending on
P
and
\sigma_{P}
,
where
d_{P}=d(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P)))
.
Since
\mathbb{E}_{P}\|\mathbf{X}\|<\infty
, this implies that under conditions (R4) and (V4),
we may interchange the order of integration and differentiation
in
\partial L_{P}/\partial\bm{\beta}
and
\partial L_{P}/\partial\bm{\gamma}
, on a neighborhood of
\bm{\xi}(P)
.
Similar to the derivation of equations (21) in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
,
it follows that besides the constraint
\log|\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})|=0
,
the pair
(\bm{\xi}(P),\lambda_{P})
satisfies

\begin{split}\int u_{1}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma_{P}}\right)\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})&=\mathbf{0}\\
\frac{1}{2\sigma_{P}}\int u_{1}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma_{P}}\right)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})\\
+\lambda\,\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{j}}\right)&=0,\end{split}

(11.15)

for
j=1,\ldots,l
,
where
u_{1}(s)=\rho_{1}^{\prime}(s)/s
and
d=d(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
, as defined by (
7.1
),
and where we abbreviate
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma})
by
\mathbf{V}
.
To solve
\lambda_{P}
from the second set of equations, we multiply the
j
-th equation by
\gamma_{j}
and then
sum over
j=1,\ldots,l
.
This leads to

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2\sigma_{P}}\int u_{1}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma_{P}}\right)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{l}\gamma_{j}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{j}}\right)\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})\\
+\lambda\,\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{l}\gamma_{j}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{j}}\right)&=0,\end{split}

which is solved by

\lambda_{P}=\frac{\displaystyle-\int u_{1}(d/\sigma_{P})(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{l}\gamma_{t}(\partial\mathbf{V}/\partial\gamma_{t})\right)\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})}{\displaystyle 2\sigma_{P}\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{l}\gamma_{t}(\partial\mathbf{V}/\partial\gamma_{t})\right)}.

When we insert this back into the second equation in (
11.15
), we find

\begin{split}&-\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{l}\gamma_{t}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{t}}\right)\int u_{1}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma_{P}}\right)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{j}}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})\\
&\quad+\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{j}}\right)\int u_{1}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma_{P}}\right)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{l}\gamma_{t}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{t}}\right)\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})=0,\end{split}

or briefly

\int u_{1}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma_{P}}\right)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{H}_{1,j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})=0,\quad j=1,\ldots,l,

(11.16)

where

\mathbf{H}_{1,j}=\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{j}}\right)\left(\sum_{t=1}^{l}\gamma_{t}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{t}}\right)-\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{l}\gamma_{t}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{t}}\right)\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{j}}.

Because
\sum_{j=1}^{l}\gamma_{j}\mathbf{H}_{1,j}=\mathbf{0}
, the system of equations (
11.16
) is linearly dependent.
Similar to Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
, we subtract the constraint from each equation.
Here, for each
j=1,\ldots,l
, we subtract the term

\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{V}}{\partial\gamma_{j}}\right)\log|\mathbf{V}|

from the left hand side of equation (
11.16
).
This finishes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2

Proof.

When
\mathbf{V}
is of the form (
7.5
), then
\partial\mathbf{V}/\partial\gamma_{j}=\mathbf{L}_{j}

and
\sum_{j=1}^{l}\gamma_{j}\partial\mathbf{V}/\partial\gamma_{j}=\mathbf{V}
.
In this case,

\mathbf{H}_{1,j}=\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{L}_{j}\right)\mathbf{V}-k\mathbf{L}_{j}
,
and
\Psi_{\bm{\gamma},j}
in Proposition
1
becomes

\begin{split}\Psi_{\bm{\gamma},j}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi},\sigma)&=\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{L}_{j}\right)\left\{u_{1}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma}\right)d^{2}-\log|\mathbf{V}|\right\}\\
&\quad-ku_{1}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma}\right)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{L}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}).\end{split}

Using that

\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{B})=\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{A})^{T}\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{B})

(11.17)

the right hand side can be written as

\begin{split}-\mathrm{vec}\left(ku_{1}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma}\right)(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}-v_{1}\left(\frac{d}{\sigma}\right)\sigma^{2}\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{V}\log|\mathbf{V}|\right)^{T}\mathrm{vec}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{L}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right),\end{split}

where
u_{1}(s)=\rho_{1}^{\prime}(s)/s
and

v_{1}(s)=u_{1}(s)s^{2}=\rho_{1}^{\prime}(s)s
.
The functions
\Psi_{\bm{\gamma},j}
, for
j=1,\ldots,l
, can be combined in one expression for the
vector valued function
\Psi_{\bm{\gamma}}
as follows.
First note that

\mathrm{vec}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{L}_{j}\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right)=\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right)\mathrm{vec}\left(\mathbf{L}_{j}\right)

for
j=1,\ldots,l
.
Then, the column vector
\Psi_{\bm{\gamma}}=(\Psi_{\bm{\gamma},1},\ldots,\Psi_{\gamma,l})
can be written as

\Psi_{\bm{\gamma}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi},\sigma)=-\mathbf{L}^{T}\left(\mathbf{V}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{-1}\right)\mathrm{vec}\left(\Psi_{\mathbf{V}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi},\sigma)\right),

with
\Psi_{\mathbf{V}}
defined in (
7.8
).
∎

11.5
Proofs for Section
8

Lemma 2
.

Suppose that
\rho_{1}
satisfies (R4) and that
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V4).
Let
\sigma(P)
be the solution of equation (
3.8
) and
let
\bm{\xi}(P)\in\mathfrak{D}
be a local minimum of
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
.
Suppose that
\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s};\sigma,P)
exists.
Let
\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})\in\mathfrak{D}

be a local minimum of
R_{P}(\bm{\beta},\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}))
with
P=P_{h,\mathbf{s}}
,
and suppose that
\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})\to\bm{\xi}(P)
, as
h\downarrow 0
.
Let
\Lambda
be defined by (
8.2
) with
\Psi
from (
7.3
)
and suppose
\Lambda
is continuously differentiable with a non-singular derivative

\mathbf{D}_{\bm{\xi}}=\partial\Lambda/\partial\bm{\xi}

and derivative
\mathbf{D}_{\sigma}=\partial\Lambda/\partial\sigma
at
(\bm{\xi}(P),\sigma(P))
.
Then for
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
,

(i)

\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s};\bm{\xi},P)=-\mathbf{D}_{\bm{\xi}}^{-1}\Big\{\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}(P),\sigma(P))+\mathbf{D}_{\sigma}\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s};\sigma,P)\Big\}
.

Let
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)
and
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})
be solutions of equation (
3.11
)
and equation (
3.11
) with
P=P_{h,\mathbf{s}}
, respectively.

(ii)

Then

\begin{split}\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s};\mathrm{vec}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1}),P)&=2\sigma(P)\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P)))\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s};\sigma,P)\\
&\qquad+\sigma^{2}(P)\frac{\partial\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}(P)))}{\partial\bm{\gamma}^{T}}\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s};\bm{\gamma},P).\end{split}

In addition, suppose that the
k^{2}\times l
matrix
\mathbf{D}_{V}=\partial\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)))/\partial\bm{\theta}^{T}
has full rank.
Then

(iii)

\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s};\bm{\theta}_{1},P)=(\mathbf{D}_{V}^{T}\mathbf{D}_{V})^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{V}^{T}\mathrm{IF}(\mathbf{s};\mathrm{vec}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1}),P)
.

Proof.

Denote
\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}}=\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})=(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}}),\bm{\gamma}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}}))

and
\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}=\sigma(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})
,
and write
\bm{\xi}_{P}=\bm{\xi}(P)
and
\sigma_{P}=\sigma(P)
.
Then
(\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})
satisfies the score equation (
7.2
) for
P
equal to
P_{h,\mathbf{s}}
.
We decompose as follows

\begin{split}0&=\int\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})\,\mathrm{d}P_{h,\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{s})\\
&=(1-h)\Lambda(\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})+h\Big(\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})-\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{P})\Big)+h\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{P}),\end{split}

(11.18)

where
\Psi
and
\Lambda
are defined by (
7.3
) and (
8.2
), respectively.
We first determine the order of
\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\bm{\xi}_{P}
, as
h\downarrow 0
.
Because
\rho_{1}
and
\mathbf{V}
satisfy (R4) and (V4), respectively, it follows that

\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})\to\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{P})
, as
h\downarrow 0
.
Because
\Lambda
is continuous differentiable at
(\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{P})
,
we have that

\begin{split}\Lambda(\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})&=\Lambda(\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})+\frac{\partial\Lambda(\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})}{\partial\bm{\xi}}(\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\bm{\xi}_{P})+o(\|\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\bm{\xi}_{P}\|)\\
&=\Lambda(\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})+\frac{\partial\Lambda(\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{P})}{\partial\bm{\xi}}(\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\bm{\xi}_{P})+o(\|\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\bm{\xi}_{P}\|)\\
&=\Lambda(\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})+\mathbf{D}_{\bm{\xi}}(\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\bm{\xi}_{P})+o(\|\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\bm{\xi}_{P}\|).\end{split}

(11.19)

In general
\Lambda(\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})\neq\mathbf{0}
.
Since
\text{IF}(\mathbf{s};\sigma,P)
exists, we find

\begin{split}\Lambda(\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})&=\Lambda(\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{P})+\frac{\partial\Lambda(\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{P})}{\partial\sigma}(\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\sigma_{P})+o(|\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\sigma_{P}|)\\
&=h\mathbf{D}_{\sigma}\text{IF}(\mathbf{s};\sigma,P)+o(h).\end{split}

(11.20)

Here we also use that
\Lambda(\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{P})=\mathbf{0}
, because
\bm{\xi}_{P}
is a solution of (
7.2
).
Inserting (
11.19
) and (
11.20
) in (
11.18
), yields

\mathbf{0}=h\mathbf{D}_{\sigma}\text{IF}(\mathbf{s};\sigma,P)+\mathbf{D}_{\bm{\xi}}(\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\bm{\xi}_{P})+h\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{P})+o(\|\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\bm{\xi}_{P}\|)+o(h).

Since
\mathbf{D}_{\bm{\xi}}
is non-singular, this means that
\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\bm{\xi}_{P}=O(h)
.
When we insert this in the previous equation and divide by
h
, we obtain

\frac{\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\bm{\xi}_{P}}{h}=-\mathbf{D}_{\bm{\xi}}^{-1}\left\{\mathbf{D}_{\sigma}\text{IF}(\mathbf{s};\sigma,P)+\Psi(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{P})\right\}+o(1).

After letting
h\downarrow 0
, this proves the first part of the lemma.

Since
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V4), it holds that

\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{h,\mathbf{s}}))-\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{P}))=\frac{\partial\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{P}))}{\partial\bm{\gamma}^{T}}(\bm{\gamma}_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\bm{\gamma}_{P})+o(\|\bm{\gamma}_{h,\mathbf{s}})-\bm{\gamma}_{P}\|).

From part (i), this means that
\text{IF}(\mathbf{s};\mathrm{vec}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}),P)
exists, and is given by

\mathrm{vec}(\text{IF}(\mathbf{s};\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}),P))=\frac{\partial\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{P}))}{\partial\bm{\gamma}^{T}}\text{IF}(\mathbf{s};\bm{\gamma},P).

Since
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)
satisfies (
3.11
),
it follows that
\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1}(P))=\sigma^{2}_{P}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{P})
,
and similarly for
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})
.
This implies that

\begin{split}&\mathrm{vec}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}}))-\mathrm{vec}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1}(P))\\
&=\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}^{2}\mathrm{vec}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{h,\mathbf{s}})-\sigma_{P}^{2}\mathrm{vec}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{P})\\
&=\mathrm{vec}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{P})\left(\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}^{2}-\sigma_{P}^{2}\right)+\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}^{2}\left(\mathrm{vec}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{h,\mathbf{s}})-\mathrm{vec}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{P})\right)\\
&=\mathrm{vec}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{P})(2\sigma_{P}+o(1))\left(\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\sigma_{P}\right)+(\sigma_{P}+o(1))\left(\mathrm{vec}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{h,\mathbf{s}})-\mathrm{vec}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\gamma}_{P})\right).\end{split}

After dividing by
h
and letting
h\downarrow 0
, this proves the second part of the lemma.

Finally, since
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V4), as before we can write

\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}}))-\mathrm{vec}(\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1}(P))=\mathbf{D}_{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})-\bm{\theta}_{1}(P))+o(\|\bm{\theta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})-\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)\|).

Because
\text{IF}(\mathbf{s};\mathrm{vec}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1}),P)
exists and
\mathbf{D}_{V}
has full rank,
it follows that
\bm{\theta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})-\bm{\theta}_{1}(P)=O(h)
.
When insert this in the previous equation, then after dividing by
h
and letting
h\downarrow 0
,
the limit exists and we obtain

\text{IF}(\mathbf{s};\mathrm{vec}\mathbf{V}(\bm{\theta}_{1}),P)=\mathbf{D}_{V}\text{IF}(\mathbf{s};\bm{\theta}_{1},P).

Because
\mathbf{D}_{V}
has full rank, we can multiply from the left with
(\mathbf{D}_{V}^{T}\mathbf{D}_{V})^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{V}^{T}
,
which proves part three.
∎

For
\bm{\zeta}=(\bm{\beta},\bm{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\bm{\Theta}
, let

\Psi_{0}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\zeta},\sigma)=\rho_{0}\left(\frac{\displaystyle\sqrt{(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta})^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})}}{\sigma}\right)-b_{0},

(11.21)

where
\bm{\Gamma}
is defined in (
3.1
), and define

\Lambda_{0}(\bm{\zeta},\sigma)=\int\Psi_{0}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\zeta},\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s}).

(11.22)

Lemma 3
.

Suppose that
\rho_{0}
satisfies (R4) and that
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V4).
Let
\bm{\zeta}_{0}=(\bm{\beta}_{0},\bm{\theta}_{0})
be the pair of initial functionals and suppose that

\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\zeta}_{0},P)
exists.
Let
\sigma(P)
and
\sigma(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})
be solutions of equation (
3.8
)
and equation (
3.8
) with
P=P_{h,\mathbf{s}}
, respectively,
and suppose that
\sigma(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})\to\sigma(P)
, as
h\downarrow 0
.
Let
\Lambda_{0}
be defined in (
11.22
) and suppose it is continuously differentiable with derivatives

D_{0,\sigma}=\partial\Lambda_{0}/\partial\sigma\neq 0

and
\mathbf{D}_{0,\bm{\zeta}}=\partial\Lambda_{0}/\partial\bm{\zeta}\in\mathbb{R}^{q+l}
at
(\bm{\zeta}_{0}(P),\sigma(P))
.
Then for
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kq}
,

\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s};\sigma,P)=-D_{0,\sigma}^{-1}\bigg\{\Psi_{0}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\zeta}_{0}(P),\sigma(P))+\mathbf{D}_{0,\bm{\zeta}}^{T}\text{\rm IF}(\mathbf{s};\bm{\zeta}_{0},P)\bigg\}.

Proof.

Denote
\bm{\zeta}_{h,\mathbf{s}}=\bm{\zeta}_{0}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})
,
\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}=\sigma(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})
,
and write
\bm{\zeta}_{0,P}=\bm{\zeta}_{0}(P)
and
\sigma_{P}=\sigma(P)
.
By definition,
\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}
satisfies equation (
3.8
) for
P
equal to
P_{h,\mathbf{s}}
.
Similar to (
11.18
), we decompose as follows

\begin{split}0=(1-h)\Lambda_{0}(\bm{\zeta}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})&+h\Big(\Psi_{0}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\zeta}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})-\Psi_{0}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\zeta}_{0,P},\sigma_{P})\Big)\\
&+h\Psi_{0}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\zeta}_{0,P},\sigma_{P}),\end{split}

(11.23)

where
\Psi_{0}
and
\Lambda_{0}
are defined by (
11.21
) and (
11.22
), respectively.
We first determine the order of
\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\sigma_{P}
, as
h\downarrow 0
.
Because
\rho_{0}
satisfies (R4) and
\mathbf{V}
satisfies (V4), it follows that

\Psi_{0}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\zeta}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})\to\Psi_{0}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\zeta}_{0,P},\sigma_{P})
, as
h\downarrow 0
.
Since
\Lambda_{0}
is continuous differentiable at
(\bm{\zeta}_{0,P},\sigma_{P})
,
similar to (
11.19
)
we have that

\begin{split}\Lambda_{0}(\bm{\zeta}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})&=\Lambda_{0}(\bm{\zeta}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{P})+D_{0,\sigma}(\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\sigma_{P})+o(\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\sigma_{P}).\end{split}

In general
\Lambda_{0}(\bm{\zeta}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{P})\neq\mathbf{0}
, so that the behavior of
\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\sigma_{P}

depends on the behavior of

\begin{split}\Lambda_{0}(\bm{\zeta}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{P})&=\Lambda_{0}(\bm{\zeta}_{0,P},\sigma_{P})+\frac{\partial\Lambda_{0}(\bm{\zeta}_{0,P},\sigma_{P})}{\partial\bm{\zeta}}(\bm{\zeta}_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\bm{\zeta}_{0,P})+o(\|\bm{\zeta}_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\bm{\zeta}_{0,P}\|)\\
&=\mathbf{D}_{0,\bm{\zeta}}^{T}(\bm{\zeta}_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\bm{\zeta}_{0,P})+o(\|\bm{\zeta}_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\bm{\zeta}_{0,P}\|).\end{split}

Here we also use that
\Lambda_{0}(\bm{\zeta}_{0,P},\sigma_{P})=0
, because
\sigma_{P}
is a solution of (
3.8
).
Because
\text{IF}(\mathbf{s};\bm{\zeta}_{0},P)
exists, we conclude that

\Lambda_{0}(\bm{\zeta}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{P})=O(h)
, and therefore

\Lambda_{0}(\bm{\zeta}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})=D_{0,\sigma}(\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\sigma_{P})+o(\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\sigma_{P})+O(h).

When we insert this in the right hand side of (
11.23
), if follows that

\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\sigma_{P}=O(h)
.
Then, again from (
11.23
), we find that

0=D_{0,\sigma}(\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\sigma_{P})+\mathbf{D}_{0,\bm{\zeta}}^{T}(\bm{\zeta}_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\bm{\zeta}_{0,P})+h\Psi_{0}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\zeta}_{0,P},\sigma_{P})+o(h).

After dividing by
h
, it follows that

\frac{\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\sigma_{P}}{h}=-D_{0,\sigma}^{-1}\left\{\mathbf{D}_{0,\bm{\zeta}}^{T}\frac{\bm{\zeta}_{h,\mathbf{s}}-\bm{\zeta}_{0,P}}{h}+\Psi_{0}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\zeta}_{0,P},\sigma_{P})\right\}+o(1).

When we let
h\downarrow 0
and use that
\text{IF}(\mathbf{s};\bm{\zeta}_{0},P)
exists,
we conclude that the limit of the left hand side exists and is given by

\text{IF}(\mathbf{s};\sigma,P)=-D_{0,\sigma}^{-1}\Big\{\mathbf{D}_{0,\bm{\zeta}}^{T}\text{IF}(\mathbf{s};\bm{\zeta}_{0},P)+\Psi_{0}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\zeta}_{0,P},\sigma_{P})\Big\}.

This proves the lemma.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5

Proof.

Denote
\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}}=\bm{\xi}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})=(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}}),\bm{\gamma}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}}))
,

\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}}=\sigma(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})
,
and write
\bm{\xi}_{P}=\bm{\xi}(P)
and
\sigma_{P}=\sigma(P)
.
Then
(\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})
satisfies the regression score equation in (
7.2
) for
P
equal to
P_{h,\mathbf{s}}
.
Similar to (
11.18
) we decompose the regression score equation (
7.2
)
as follows

\begin{split}\mathbf{0}&=\int\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})\,\mathrm{d}P_{h,\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{s})\\
&=(1-h)\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})+h\Big(\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})-\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{P})\Big)\\
&\phantom{=(1-h)\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})}+h\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{P}),\end{split}

(11.24)

where
\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}
and
\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}
are defined by (
7.3
) and (
8.2
), respectively.
Because
\rho_{1}
and
\mathbf{V}
satisfy (R4) and (V1), respectively, it follows that

\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})\to\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{P})
, as
h\downarrow 0
.
Because
\partial\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}/\partial\bm{\beta}
is continuous at
(\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{P})

and

(\bm{\gamma}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})\to(\bm{\gamma}_{P},\sigma_{P})
, we find that

\begin{split}\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})&=\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})+\left(\mathbf{D}_{\bm{\beta}}+o(1)\right)(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})-\bm{\beta}_{1}(P)).\end{split}

Because
\bm{\beta}_{1}(P)
is a point of symmetry and
\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}
is an
odd function of
\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta}
,
it follows that
\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P),\bm{\gamma}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})=\mathbf{0}
.
This means that

\Lambda_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\xi}_{h,\mathbf{s}},\sigma_{h,\mathbf{s}})=\mathbf{D}_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})-\bm{\beta}_{1}(P))+o(\|\bm{\beta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})-\bm{\beta}_{1}(P)\|).

Together with (
11.24
), we find that

\mathbf{0}=\mathbf{D}_{\bm{\beta}}(\bm{\beta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})-\bm{\beta}_{1}(P))+h\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{P})+o(\|\bm{\beta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})-\bm{\beta}_{1}(P)\|)+o(h).

Since
\mathbf{D}_{\bm{\beta}}
is non-singular, this implies that
\bm{\beta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})-\bm{\beta}_{1}(P)=O(h)
.
When we insert this in the previous equation and divide by
h
, we obtain

\frac{\bm{\beta}_{1}(P_{h,\mathbf{s}})-\bm{\beta}_{1}(P)}{h}=-\mathbf{D}_{\bm{\beta}}^{-1}\Psi_{\bm{\beta}}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\xi}_{P},\sigma_{P})+o(1).

When we let
h\downarrow 0
, this finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4
.

Let
\Lambda_{0}
be defined by (
11.22
) with
\Psi_{0}
defined in (
11.21
)
and suppose that
\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}\|<\infty
.
Suppose that
\rho_{0}
and
\mathbf{V}
satisfy (R2), (R4) and (V4), respectively.
Let
\bm{\zeta}_{0}(P)=(\bm{\beta}_{0}(P),\bm{\theta}_{0}(P))
be the pair of initial functionals
and let
\sigma(P)
be a solution of (
3.8
).
Let
N\subset\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\bm{\Theta}\times(0,\infty)
be an open neighborhood of
(\bm{\zeta}_{0}(P),\sigma(P))
.
Then
\Lambda_{0}
is continuous differentiable at
(\bm{\zeta}_{0}(P),\sigma(P))
and
for all
(\bm{\zeta},\sigma)\in N
,

\frac{\partial\Lambda_{0}(\bm{\zeta},\sigma)}{\partial\bm{\zeta}}=\int\frac{\partial\Psi_{0}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\zeta},\sigma)}{\partial\bm{\zeta}}\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s})\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial\Lambda_{0}(\bm{\zeta},\sigma)}{\partial\sigma}=\int\frac{\partial\Psi_{0}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\zeta},\sigma)}{\partial\sigma}\,\mathrm{d}P(\mathbf{s}).

Proof.

Let
(\bm{\zeta},\sigma)\in N
.
Consider
\bm{\zeta}\mapsto\Lambda_{0}(\bm{\zeta},\sigma)
with
\sigma\in(0,\infty)
fixed.
From (
11.21
) we find

\frac{\partial\Psi_{0}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\zeta},\sigma)}{\partial\bm{\beta}}=\rho^{\prime}_{0}\left(\frac{d_{\Gamma}}{\sigma}\right)\frac{1}{2d_{\Gamma}\sigma}\mathbf{X}^{T}\bm{\Gamma}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})=\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}u_{0}\left(\frac{d_{\Gamma}}{\sigma}\right)\mathbf{X}^{T}\bm{\Gamma}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})

where
u_{0}(s)=\rho_{0}(s)/s
and
d_{\Gamma}^{2}=(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})^{T}\bm{\Gamma}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})
,
and where we write
\bm{\Gamma}
for
\bm{\Gamma}(\bm{\theta})
, as defined in (
3.1
).
Similar to the proof of Lemma 11.2 in Lopuhaä
et al

[
22
]
we obtain

\left\|\mathbf{X}^{T}\bm{\Gamma}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\beta})\right\|^{2}\leq d_{\Gamma}^{2}\|\mathbf{X}\|^{2}\lambda_{1}(\bm{\Gamma}^{-1}).

This means that
